Do not share this medicine with another person, even if they have the same symptoms you have. Sharing a bottle can spread germs.

Your dose will depend on the fluticasone brand or strength you use , and your dose may change once your symptoms improve. Follow all dosing instructions very carefully.

- A child using the nasal spray should be supervised by an adult.

Read and carefully follow any Instructions for Use provided with your medicine. Ask your doctor or pharmacist if you do not understand these instructions.

Shake the bottle before each use. Flonase Allergy Relief should be shaken gently, and Flonase Sensimist should be shaken vigorously.

Use this product only once a day and do not use more than directed. Avoid getting the spray in your eyes or mouth.

If you switched to fluticasone from another steroid medicine, you should not stop using it suddenly. Follow your doctor's instructions about tapering your dose.

Storage differs between products. Store Flonase Allergy Relief and Children's Flonase Allergy Relief between 4°C and 30°C (39°F and 86°F). Store Flonase Sensimist between 15°C and 30°C (59°F and 86°F), and do not refrigerate or freeze it.

Throw the bottle away once you have used the number of sprays stated on the carton, even if there is still liquid in the bottle. The correct amount of medicine in each spray cannot be assured after the labeled number of sprays has been used. Bottle sizes vary between products and pack sizes, ranging from 30 to 144 metered sprays.

### Dosing information

### Usual Adult Dose for Rhinitis (adults and children 12 years of age and older):

Flonase Allergy Relief Nasal Spray (fluticasone propionate 50 mcg per spray):

- Week 1: use 2 sprays in each nostril once daily
- Week 2 through 6 months: use 1 or 2 sprays in each nostril once daily, as needed to treat your symptoms
- After 6 months of daily use: ask your doctor if you can keep using this medicine

Flonase Sensimist Nasal Spray (fluticasone furoate 27.5 mcg per spray):

- Week 1: use 2 sprays in each nostril once daily
- Week 2 through 6 months: use 1 or 2 sprays in each nostril once daily, as needed to treat your symptoms
- After 6 months of daily use: ask your doctor if you can keep using this medicine

### Usual Pediatric Dose for Rhinitis:

- 4 to 11 years: Flonase Allergy Relief or Children's Flonase Allergy Relief (fluticasone propionate 50 mcg per spray): 1 spray in each nostril once daily.
- 2 to 11 years: Flonase Sensimist or Children's Flonase Sensimist (fluticasone furoate 27.5 mcg per spray): 1 spray in each nostril once daily.
- Under 4 years: do not use fluticasone propionate nasal spray.
- Under 2 years: do not use fluticasone furoate nasal spray.

An adult should supervise a child using the spray. Children should use it for the shortest amount of time necessary to achieve symptom relief. Talk to your child's doctor if your child needs to use the spray for longer than two months a year.

### What happens if I miss a dose?

Use the medicine as soon as you can, but skip the missed dose if it is almost time for your next dose. Do not use two doses at one time.

### What happens if I overdose?

Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222.

An overdose of Flonase is not expected to produce life-threatening symptoms. Using more than the recommended dose, or using it for longer than directed, may increase the chance of corticosteroid-related side effects such as cataracts or glaucoma , adrenal gland problems, an increased risk of infection, sores inside the nose, or slower growth in children.

Flonase Nasal Spray — What should I avoid while using Flonase?
Avoid getting the spray in your eyes or mouth. If this does happen, rinse with water.

Avoid being near people who are sick or have infections. Call your doctor for preventive treatment if you are exposed to chickenpox or measles .

Flonase Nasal Spray — Flonase side effects
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction to Flonase: hives , rash; feeling light-headed; difficult breathing; swelling of your face, lips, tongue, or throat.

Call your doctor at once if you have:

- severe or frequent nosebleeds;
- a constant whistling sound from your nose, which may be a sign of damage inside your nose;
- noisy breathing, runny nose, or crusting around your nostrils;
- redness, sores, or white patches in your mouth or throat;
- fever, chills, body aches;
- new changes to your vision, blurred vision, eye pain, or seeing halos around lights;
- any wound that will not heal; or
- signs of a hormonal disorder: worsening tiredness or muscle weakness, feeling light-headed, nausea , vomiting .

Steroid medicine can affect growth in children. Tell your doctor if your child is not growing at a normal rate while using this medicine.

Common Flonase side effects may include:

- minor nosebleed, burning or itching in your nose;
- stinging or sneezing for a few seconds right after use;
- sores or white patches inside or around your nose;
- cough, trouble breathing;
- headache , back pain ;
- sinus pain, sore throat , fever; or
- nausea, vomiting.

This is not a complete list of side effects, and others may occur. Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects. You may report side effects to FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.

Flonase Nasal Spray — What other drugs will affect Flonase?
Tell your doctor or pharmacist about all your other medicines, especially:

- an antifungal medicine, such as ketoconazole tablets;
- an antiviral medicine to treat hepatitis C or HIV/AIDS , such as ritonavir; or
- another steroid medicine for asthma, allergies or skin rash.

This list is not complete. Other drugs may interact with fluticasone nasal, including prescription and over-the-counter medicines, vitamins , and herbal products . Not all possible drug interactions are listed here.

Fluoxetine — What is Fluoxetine?
Fluoxetine is a prescription antidepressant used to treat major depressive disorder, obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD), bulimia nervosa, and panic disorder. When used together with olanzapine (Zyprexa), it is also used to treat depressive episodes associated with bipolar I disorder and treatment-resistant depression.

Fluoxetine belongs to a class of antidepressants called selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs).

The original brand name of fluoxetine is Prozac, and it's now available as a generic.

Fluoxetine — How does fluoxetine (Prozac) work?
Fluoxetine works by blocking the reabsorption (reuptake) of serotonin in the brain, leaving more of it active between nerve cells. Serotonin is a neurotransmitter that helps regulate mood, emotions, and sleep. By increasing the availability of serotonin, fluoxetine can relieve symptoms of depression, anxiety, and OCD.

Fluoxetine belongs to a class of antidepressants called selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs) . SSRIs are one of the most commonly prescribed antidepressant classes because they are generally well-tolerated and effective across a range of conditions.

Most people need to take fluoxetine for 4 to 6 weeks before feeling the full effects.

Fluoxetine — Fluoxetine Side Effects
Like all medications, Prozac can cause side effects. Not everyone experiences them, and many improve after the first few weeks as your body adjusts to the medication. Tell your doctor if any side effects bother you or do not go away.

### Common Fluoxetine Side Effects

The most commonly reported side effects of fluoxetine (Prozac) include:

- Nausea
- Trouble sleeping (insomnia)
- Drowsiness or sleepiness
- Anxiety or feeling nervous or on edge
- Trembling or shaking (tremor)
- Excessive sweating
- Loss of appetite
- Diarrhea
- Dry mouth
- Indigestion or upset stomach
- Weakness or fatigue
- Sore throat
- Sinus congestion or infection
- Flu-like symptoms
- Skin rash
- Flushing or feeling of warmth
- Yawning
- Unusual or vivid dreams
- Decreased sex drive
- Difficulty getting or keeping an erection
- Delayed or abnormal ejaculation

### Serious side effects

Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction to this medicine ( hives , difficult breathing, swelling in your face or throat) or a severe skin reaction (fever, sore throat , burning eyes, skin pain, red or purple skin rash with blistering and peeling).

Report any new or worsening symptoms to your doctor, such as mood or behavior changes, anxiety , panic attacks , trouble sleeping, or if you feel impulsive, irritable, agitated, hostile, aggressive, restless, hyperactive (mentally or physically), more depressed, or have thoughts about suicide or hurting yourself.

This medicine may cause serious side effects. Call your doctor at once if you have:

- blurred vision, tunnel vision, eye pain or swelling, or seeing halos around lights;
- fast or pounding heartbeats, fluttering in your chest, shortness of breath, and sudden dizziness (like you might pass out);
- low levels of sodium in the body - headache , confusion, slurred speech, severe weakness, vomiting , loss of coordination, feeling unsteady; or
- severe nervous system reaction - very stiff (rigid) muscles, high fever, sweating, confusion, fast or uneven heartbeats, tremors, feeling like you might pass out.

Seek medical attention right away if you have symptoms of serotonin syndrome, such as: agitation, hallucinations, fever, sweating, shivering, fast heart rate , muscle stiffness, twitching, loss of coordination, nausea , vomiting, or diarrhea .

This is not a complete list of side effects, and others may occur. Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects. You may report side effects to the FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.

Fluoxetine — Who Should Not Take This Medicine?
Do not take fluoxetine if any of the following apply to you. Tell your doctor or pharmacist about all medications you currently take or have recently stopped taking.

Do not take fluoxetine if you:

- Are allergic to fluoxetine or any of its ingredients
- Currently take a monoamine oxidase inhibitor (MAOI), including the antibiotic linezolid. If you are unsure whether your medication is an MAOI, ask your doctor or pharmacist
- Stopped taking an MAOI in the last 2 weeks
- Currently take pimozide (Orap) as this combination can cause a serious, potentially life-threatening irregular heart rhythm
- Currently taking thioridazine (Mellaril) as this combination can cause a dangerous irregular heart rhythm

Important timing rules with MAOIs and thioridazine:

- Wait at least 2 weeks after stopping an MAOI before starting fluoxetine
- Wait at least 5 weeks after stopping fluoxetine before starting an MAOI or thioridazine

Fluoxetine — Warnings
Like all medications, Prozac carries some important warnings you should be aware of before and during treatment. Contact your doctor right away if you experience any of the following.

### Boxed warning

Fluoxetine carries a boxed warning, the FDA's most serious warning, regarding suicidal thoughts and behavior. Antidepressants, including fluoxetine, may increase the risk of suicidal thoughts in children, adolescents, and young adult s , particularly in the first few months of treatment or when your dose is changed. This risk was not seen in adults over 24, and adults 65 and older actually showed a reduced risk.

Anyone starting this medicine should be closely monitored for worsening mood or new or worsening suicidal thoughts, and families and caregivers should stay in regular contact with the prescriber.

### Other warnings

- Serotonin syndrome: A potentially life-threatening condition that can occur when serotonin builds up to dangerous levels. This can occur when taken alone, but is more likely when taken with other serotonergic medications. Seek emergency care immediately if you experience agitation, hallucinations, rapid heartbeat, high fever, muscle rigidity, tremor, or seizures.
- Severe allergic reactions: Stop taking this medicine and seek emergency help if you develop trouble breathing, facial swelling, hives, or blistering rash.
- Abnormal bleeding: This medicine may increase the risk of bleeding or bruising, particularly if taken with blood thinners (such as warfarin), NSAIDs (such as ibuprofen or naproxen), or aspirin.
- Eye problems (angle-closure glaucoma): This medicine can cause angle-closure glaucoma with symptoms of eye pain, vision changes, or swelling around the eye in some people. An eye exam before starting treatment may be recommended for some people to assess their risk.
- Seizures: Tell your doctor if you have a history of seizures before starting this medicine, or have conditions that potentially increase your risk of seizures
- Manic episodes Fluoxetine may trigger mania, including racing thoughts, reckless behavior, drastically reduced need for sleep, and unusual bursts of energy or irritability.
- Changes in appetite or weight: Weight and appetite changes can occur, with some patients having significant weight loss. Children and adolescents should have their height and weight monitored regularly during treatment.
- Low sodium (hyponatremia): Low blood sodium levels can occur, particularly in older adults. Symptoms include headache, weakness, confusion, and difficulty concentrating.
- Heart rhythm changes (QT prolongation): Fluoxetine can affect the heart's electrical activity, potentially causing a serious and life-threatening irregular heartbeat. Seek medical attention if you experience a fast, slow, or irregular heartbeat, shortness of breath, or fainting.
- Sexual dysfunction: SSRIs, including fluoxetine, commonly affect sexual function. In men, this may include delayed or absent ejaculation or erectile problems. In women, it may include decreased libido or difficulty reaching orgasm. Talk to your doctor if this occurs, as treatment options are available.
- Drowsiness and impaired concentration: This medicine may affect your ability to think clearly, make decisions, or react quickly. Use caution when driving or operating machinery until you know how this medication affects you.
- Anxiety and Insomnia: May occur in some patients while taking this medicine.
- Blood sugar changes: People with diabetes may experience low blood sugar while taking this medicine, and high blood sugar when it is stopped. Your doctor may need to adjust your diabetes medications.

Fluoxetine — How should I take fluoxetine?
Take fluoxetine exactly as prescribed by your doctor. Follow all directions on your prescription label and read all medication guides or instruction sheets. Your doctor may occasionally change your dose.

Swallow the delayed-release capsule whole and do not crush, chew, break, or open it.

Measure liquid medicine carefully. Use the dosing syringe provided, or use a medicine dose-measuring device (not a kitchen spoon).

It may take up to 4 weeks before your symptoms improve. Keep using the medication as directed and tell your doctor if your symptoms do not improve.

Tell your doctor if you have any changes in sexual function, such as loss of interest in sex, trouble having an orgasm, or (in men) problems with erections or ejaculation. Some sexual problems can be treated.

Do not stop using fluoxetine suddenly, or you could have unpleasant withdrawal symptoms. Ask your doctor how to safely stop using fluoxetine.

Store at room temperature away from moisture and heat.

Fluoxetine — Fluoxetine Dosing Information
Fluoxetine (Prozac) is taken orally, and your doctor will determine the right dose for you based on your condition, age, and response to treatment. Always follow your doctor's instructions, do not change your dose or stop taking this medicine without speaking to them first.

### Major Depressive Disorder (MDD)

Adults: Treatment typically starts at 20 mg once daily in the morning. Your doctor may increase the dose after several weeks if needed. The maximum dose is 80 mg/day.

Children and adolescents (aged 8 and older): Treatment starts at 10 mg or 20 mg per day. In lower-weight children, the starting dose and target dose may remain at 10 mg/day.

With all patients, the full effect of fluoxetine for depression may take 4 weeks or longer.

### Obsessive Compulsive Disorder (OCD)

Adult dose: Treatment starts at 20 mg once daily in the morning and may be increased after several weeks if there is a lack of clinical improvement. The recommended dose range is 20 mg to 60 mg per day. Full therapeutic effect may take 5 weeks or longer.

Adolescents and higher-weight children (aged 7 and older): Treatment starts at 10 mg/day, increasing to 20 mg/day after 2 weeks. Additional dose increases may be considered after several more weeks if insufficient clinical improvement is observed. The recommended dose range is 20 mg to 60 mg per day.

Lower-weight children: Treatment starts at 10 mg/day with dose increases after several more weeks if there is a lack of clinical improvement. The recommended dose range of 20 mg to 30 mg per day, although experience with daily doses greater than 20 mg is very minimal,

### Bulimia Nervosa

Adult dose: The recommended dose is 60 mg once daily in the morning. Your doctor may gradually increase your dose to this level over several days. Clinical trials showed that the 60 mg dose was effective in reducing binge-eating and purging behaviors.

### Panic Disorder

Adult dose: Treatment starts at 10 mg/day, increasing to 20 mg/day after one week. Your doctor may adjust the dose after several weeks if needed. Clinical trials used a dose range of 10 mg/day to 60 mg/day, but the most commonly used dose in clinical trials was 20 mg/day.

#### General Dosing Notes

- Fluoxetine is usually taken in the morning to minimize the risk of sleep disturbances
- It may take several weeks to feel the full benefit of fluoxetine, do not stop taking it without talking to your doctor first
- Your doctor will periodically reassess whether you need to continue treatment
- Older adults may need a lower dose or may take fluoxetine less frequently than younger adults
- People with liver problems may also require a lower or less frequent dose
- If you have other medical conditions or take multiple medications, your doctor may need to adjust your fluoxetine dose accordingly

Fluoxetine — What happens if I miss a dose?
Take the medicine as soon as you can, but skip the missed dose if it is almost time for your next dose. Do not take two doses at one time.

Fluoxetine — What happens if I overdose?
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222.

Fluoxetine — What to avoid
Drinking alcohol can increase certain side effects of fluoxetine.

Avoid driving or hazardous activity until you know how fluoxetine will affect you. Your reactions could be impaired.

Fluoxetine — What other drugs will affect fluoxetine?
Using fluoxetine with other drugs that make you drowsy can worsen this effect. Ask your doctor before using opioid medication, a sleeping pill, a muscle relaxer, or medicine for anxiety or seizures.

Ask your doctor before taking a nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug (NSAID) such as aspirin , ibuprofen ( Advil , Motrin ), naproxen ( Aleve ), celecoxib ( Celebrex ), diclofenac , indomethacin , meloxicam , and others. Using an NSAID with fluoxetine may cause you to bruise or bleed easily.

Fluoxetine can cause a serious heart problem, and your risk may be higher if you also use certain other medicines for infections, asthma , heart problems, high blood pressure , depression, mental illness, cancer, malaria , or HIV .

Tell your doctor about all your current medicines. Many drugs can affect fluoxetine, especially:

- any other antidepressant;
- St. John's Wort ;
- tryptophan (sometimes called L-tryptophan);
- a blood thinner - warfarin , Coumadin , Jantoven ;
- medicine to treat anxiety, mood disorders, thought disorders, or mental illness - amitriptyline , buspirone , desipramine , lithium , nortriptyline , and many others;
- medicine to treat ADHD or narcolepsy - Adderall , Concerta , Ritalin , Vyvanse , Zenzedi , and others;
- migraine headache medicine - rizatriptan , sumatriptan , zolmitriptan , and others; or
- narcotic pain medicine - fentanyl , tramadol .

This list is not complete and many other drugs may affect fluoxetine. This includes prescription and over-the-counter medicines, vitamins , and herbal products . Not all possible drug interactions are listed here.

Furosemide — What is furosemide?
Furosemide is a loop diuretic used to treat fluid retention (edema) in people with congestive heart failure, liver disease, or a chronic kidney disorder such as nephrotic syndrome. Furosemide is also used to treat high blood pressure (hypertension).

Furosemide works by increasing the amount of urine the body makes, which helps reduce swelling and symptoms of fluid retention and helps lower high blood pressure. Furosemide tablets are sometimes called water pills as they increase how much you urinate.

Furosemide is also known as frusemide in some countries. It has been available in the United States since the 1960s and comes as tablets (Lasix), an oral liquid, an intravenous solution, and subcutaneous injections and infusions that can be given at home (Furoscix, Lasix ONYU).

Furosemide — Furosemide side effects
### Common side effects

Common furosemide side effects may include:

- diarrhea, constipation, loss of appetite
- numbness or tingling
- headache, dizziness
- blurred vision
- redness, bruising, swelling, or pain where a subcutaneous dose is given.

### Serious furosemide side effects

Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction to furosemide (hives, difficult breathing, swelling in your face or throat) or a severe skin reaction (fever, sore throat, burning eyes, skin pain, red or purple skin rash with blistering and peeling).

Furosemide may cause other serious side effects. Call your doctor at once if you have:

- a light-headed feeling, like you might pass out
- ringing in your ears, hearing loss
- muscle spasms or contractions
- pale skin, easy bruising, unusual bleeding
- high blood sugar, with increased thirst, increased urination, dry mouth, or fruity breath odor
- kidney problems, with swelling, urinating less, feeling tired or short of breath
- signs of liver or pancreas problems, such as loss of appetite, upper stomach pain that may spread to your back, nausea or vomiting, dark urine, or jaundice (yellowing of the skin or eyes)
- signs of an electrolyte imbalance, such as increased thirst or urination, constipation, muscle weakness, leg cramps, numbness or tingling, feeling jittery, or fluttering in your chest.

This is not a complete list of side effects, and others may occur. Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects. You may report side effects to the FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.

Furosemide — Warnings
You should not use furosemide if you are unable to urinate.

Using more than your recommended dose will not make this medicine more effective. High doses of this medicine may cause irreversible hearing loss.

Before using this medicine, tell your doctor if you have kidney disease, enlarged prostate, urination problems, cirrhosis or other liver disease, an electrolyte imbalance, high cholesterol, gout, lupus, diabetes, or an allergy to sulfa drugs.

Tell your doctor if you have recently had an MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) or any type of scan using a radioactive dye that is injected into your veins. Do not take more of this medication than is recommended.

If you are being treated for high blood pressure, keep using this medicine even if you feel fine. High blood pressure often has no symptoms.

Furosemide is a potent diuretic which, if given in excessive amounts, can lead to a profound diuresis with water and electrolyte depletion. Careful medical supervision is required, and the dose schedule must be adjusted to the individual patient's needs.

Tell your doctor about all your other medicines. Some drugs should not be used with furosemide.

Furosemide — Before taking this medicine
You should not use furosemide if you are allergic to it or are unable to urinate.

To make sure furosemide is safe for you, tell your doctor if you have ever had:

- an electrolyte imbalance (such as low levels of potassium or magnesium in your blood)
- enlarged prostate, bladder obstruction, or other urination problems
- gout
- lupus
- diabetes
- an allergy to sulfa drugs
- kidney disease
- cirrhosis or other liver disease.

Tell your doctor if you have an MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) or any type of scan using a radioactive dye that is injected into a vein. Contrast dyes and furosemide can harm your kidneys.

### Subcutaneous furosemide given at home

Two brands of furosemide are designed to be given under the skin at home rather than in hospital.

Furoscix is used to treat edema in adults with chronic heart failure or chronic kidney disease, including the nephrotic syndrome, and in children weighing 43 kg (94.7 lb) or more. It comes in two forms:

- the Furoscix ReadyFlow, a prefilled autoinjector that delivers 80 mg of furosemide in about 10 seconds. It can be used in adults and in children weighing 43 kg or more.
- the Furoscix On-Body Infusor, a wearable device that delivers 30 mg of furosemide over the first hour and then 12.5 mg per hour for the next 4 hours, giving 80 mg over about 5 hours. It can be used in adults and in children weighing 43 kg or more.

Do not use the Furoscix On-Body Infusor if you are allergic to medical adhesives. The Furoscix ReadyFlow contains benzyl alcohol as a preservative.

Lasix ONYU is a pre-programmed infusor containing furosemide that is FDA-approved for the treatment of edema in adults with chronic heart failure.

Subcutaneous furosemide is not for use in an emergency, and it is not for long-term use. It should be replaced with oral diuretics as soon as practical.

### Pregnancy

It is not known if furosemide will harm an unborn baby. Tell your doctor if you are pregnant or plan to become pregnant.

### Breastfeeding

It may not be safe to breastfeed while using furosemide. Ask your doctor about any risk. Furosemide may slow breast milk production.

Furosemide — How should I use furosemide?
Take or administer furosemide exactly as prescribed by your doctor. Follow all directions on your prescription label and read all medication guides or instruction sheets.

Furosemide oral tablets and liquid are taken by mouth. Measure liquid medicine with the supplied measuring device, not a kitchen spoon.

Furosemide injection is given in a muscle, under the skin, or in a vein. A healthcare provider will give you this injection if you are unable to take the medicine by mouth.

### Using subcutaneous furosemide at home

You or your caregiver should be trained before your first dose, and you should read the Instructions for Use that comes with your prescription. Wash your hands first, and check that the liquid is clear and colorless to slightly yellow. Do not use it if you see particles or if it is cloudy or discolored.

Subcutaneous furosemide is given into the abdomen only, and you should use a different spot on the abdomen each time.

Furoscix ReadyFlow autoinjector

- Choose a site at least two inches from your belly button, avoiding skin that is broken or irritated and any scars or stretch marks. Clean it and let it dry.
- Pull the blue cap straight off, then press the device firmly against your skin and hold it there for about 10 seconds, until the yellow plunger rod stops moving and fills the viewing window.
- If you lift the device away before the window is completely yellow, you may not get the full dose. Call your healthcare provider if this happens, and do not use a second device unless you are told to.

Furoscix On-Body Infusor and Lasix ONYU Infusor

- The infusion takes about 5 hours, so plan to stay somewhere you can relax and reach a bathroom.
- Apply the device to a flat, clean, hairless area of the abdomen below the rib cage and above the belt line, and press it firmly into place. Do not remove and reapply it.
- Limit your activity while wearing it and avoid bending as much as you can. Do not exercise or do anything that might knock it off.
- Do not get the infusor wet. Do not bathe, shower, or swim while wearing it, and do not apply lotions or creams to the area where it is placed.
- Traveling by car or airplane while wearing the infusor is not recommended, and the Furoscix On-Body Infusor cannot be used in an MRI setting.
- Do not use the infusor within 12 inches of mobile phones, tablets, computers, or wireless accessories such as a remote control or Bluetooth device.
- Your healthcare provider will show you what the indicator light and alarms mean. You must use the infusor in a setting where you can detect and respond to these alarms to ensure you receive the full dose. If the device falls off, or the light blinks red, call your healthcare provider and do not reuse it.

Dispose of used autoinjectors, infusors, and cartridges in a sharps container. Do not put them in household trash and do not recycle them.

### General furosemide dosing information

Do not use more than your recommended dose. High doses of furosemide may cause irreversible hearing loss.

Doses are based on weight in children and teenagers. Your child's dose may change if the child gains or loses weight.

This medicine will make you urinate more often, and you may get dehydrated easily. Follow your doctor's instructions about using potassium supplements or getting enough salt and potassium in your diet.

Your blood pressure will need to be checked often, and you may need other medical tests.

If you have high blood pressure, keep using furosemide even if you feel well. High blood pressure often has no symptoms.

If you need surgery, tell the surgeon ahead of time that you are using furosemide.

Furosemide — Dosing information
### Usual adult dose for edema associated with congestive heart failure, cirrhosis of the liver, and chronic renal disease (including nephrotic syndrome)

Oral: initial dose 20 to 80 mg orally once. This may be repeated at the same dose or increased by 20 or 40 mg no sooner than 6 to 8 hours after the previous dose, until the desired diuretic effect has been obtained.

Maintenance dose: administer the dose that provided the desired diuretic effect once or twice a day (for example, at 8 am and 2 pm).

Comments: edema may be most efficiently and safely mobilized by giving this drug on 2 to 4 consecutive days each week. When doses greater than 80 mg/day are given for prolonged periods, careful clinical observation and laboratory monitoring are particularly advisable.

### Usual Furoscix adult and pediatric 43 kg and above dose (ReadyFlow autoinjector)

- 80 mg of furosemide given subcutaneously in about 10 seconds.

### Usual Furoscix adult and pediatric 43 kg and above dose (On-Body Infusor)

- 30 mg of furosemide over the first hour, then 12.5 mg per hour for the subsequent 4 hours. This dose is delivered automatically by the On-Body Infusor.
- In children weighing at least 43 kg, the device must be applied by a healthcare provider or adult caregiver.

Furoscix is not for chronic use and should be replaced with oral diuretics as soon as practical. People with significantly reduced kidney function may need additional diuretic therapy, because both Furoscix devices deliver a fixed 80 mg dose.

### Usual adult furosemide dose for pulmonary edema

IV: 40 mg IV slowly over 1 to 2 minutes. If a satisfactory response does not occur within one hour, this may be increased to 80 mg IV slowly over 1 to 2 minutes.

Use: adjunctive therapy in acute pulmonary edema. IV administration is indicated when a rapid onset of diuresis is desired.

### Usual adult furosemide dose for hypertension

Oral: initial dose 80 mg/day, usually divided into 40 mg orally twice a day.

Use: treatment of hypertension alone or in combination with other antihypertensive agents.

### Usual pediatric furosemide oral dose for edema

Initial dose: 2 mg/kg orally once. If the diuretic response to the initial dose is not satisfactory, this may be increased by 1 or 2 mg/kg, given no sooner than 6 to 8 hours after the previous dose. Doses greater than 6 mg/kg body weight are not recommended.

Maintenance dose: adjust to the minimum effective dose.

Use: treatment of edema associated with congestive heart failure, cirrhosis of the liver and renal disease, including the nephrotic syndrome, especially when an agent with greater diuretic potential is desired.

Furosemide — What happens if I miss a dose?
Furosemide is sometimes used only once, so you may not be on a dosing schedule. If you are using the medication regularly, use the medicine as soon as you can, but skip the missed dose if it is almost time for your next dose. Do not use two doses at one time.

Furosemide — What happens if I overdose?
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222.

Overdose symptoms may include feeling very thirsty or hot, heavy sweating, hot and dry skin, extreme weakness, or fainting.

Furosemide — What should I avoid while using furosemide?
Avoid getting up too fast from a sitting or lying position, or you may feel dizzy.

Avoid becoming dehydrated. Follow your doctor's instructions about the type and amount of liquids you should drink while you are using furosemide.

Drinking alcohol with this medicine can cause side effects.

Furosemide could make you sunburn more easily. Avoid sunlight or tanning beds. Wear protective clothing and use sunscreen (SPF 30 or higher) when you are outdoors.

If you have high blood pressure, ask a doctor or pharmacist before taking any medicines that can raise your blood pressure, such as diet pills or cough-and-cold medicine.

Furosemide — What other drugs will affect furosemide?
Sometimes it is not safe to use certain medicines at the same time. Some drugs can affect your blood levels of other drugs you use, which may increase side effects or make the medicines less effective.

If you also take sucralfate, take your furosemide dose 2 hours before or 2 hours after you take sucralfate.

Tell your doctor about all your other medicines, especially:

- another diuretic (do not use furosemide with ethacrynic acid)
- methotrexate
- chloral hydrate
- lithium
- phenytoin
- an antibiotic, especially a cephalosporin or an aminoglycoside (avoid using aminoglycosides with furosemide)
- cancer medicine, such as cisplatin
- cyclosporine
- thyroid hormones
- heart or blood pressure medicine, including ACE inhibitors and angiotensin receptor blockers
- NSAIDs (nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs), such as aspirin, ibuprofen (Advil, Motrin), naproxen (Aleve), celecoxib, diclofenac, indomethacin, meloxicam, and others.

This list is not complete. Other drugs may interact with furosemide, including prescription and over-the-counter medicines, vitamins, and herbal products. Not all possible drug interactions are listed here.

Gabapentin — What is gabapentin?
Gabapentin is a prescription medicine used to treat partial seizures , nerve pain from shingles ( postherpetic neuralgia ), and restless legs syndrome (Horizant brand only). It may also be prescribed off-label for other conditions including diabetic nerve pain, anxiety, and fibromyalgia. Gabapentin is thought to work by calming overactive nerve signals in the brain and nervous system. It is an anticonvulsant and nerve pain medication from the drug class gabapentinoids

Gabapentin brand names are Neurontin, Gralise, Horizant, and Relgaabi, which may not be interchangeable.

Gabapentin is not a federally controlled substance, but some states classify it as Schedule V and others require gabapentin prescriptions to be reported to their prescription drug monitoring program (PDMP). Rules on refills and quantity limits vary by state.

The most common side effects of gabapentin are dizziness, drowsiness (somnolence), and swelling in the hands or feet (peripheral edema). These often improve as your body adjusts, but do not drive until you know how gabapentin affects you.

Gabapentin — How does gabapentin work?
Gabapentin works by calming overactive electrical signals in the brain and nervous system. It does this by binding to the alpha-2-delta subunit of calcium channels in nerve cells, which is thought to normalize chemical signals nerves send to each other and results in a calmer, less excitable nerve circuit.

That same effect helps in different ways depending on what gabapentin is treating:

- Epilepsy - dampens the runaway electrical firing that causes seizures.
- Postherpetic neuralgia (shingles nerve pain) - reduces the abnormal pain signals from nerves damaged by shingles.
- Restless legs syndrome (gabapentin enacarbil / Horizant) - the exact mechanism is less clear, but the same calming effect on overactive nerves is thought to ease the urge-to-move sensation.

Gabapentin is not an opioid, sedative, or anti-inflammatory painkiller. It works directly on the nerves, which is why it helps nerve-related pain and why it isn't useful for everyday pain like headaches or sprains. Most people need 1 to 2 weeks of regular dosing for the full effect to build up.

Gabapentin — Warnings
Respiratory depression. Gabapentin can cause life-threatening breathing problems (respiratory depression) , especially if you already have a breathing disorder (such as COPD), are elderly, or if you use other medicines that can make you drowsy or slow your breathing, such as opioids or CNS depressants. Seek emergency medical attention if you have very slow breathing.

Suicidal thoughts or behaviors. Some people have thoughts about suicide while taking and after discontinuing this medicine. Stay alert to changes in your mood or symptoms. Tell your doctor right away if you have any sudden changes in mood or behavior, or thoughts about suicide.

Abrupt or rapid discontinuation. Seizures may occur if you stop using gabapentin suddenly. Ask your doctor before stopping the medicine.  Stopping abruptly can cause withdrawal symptoms such as anxiety, trouble sleeping, nausea, pain, and sweating.

Sleepiness, sedation, and dizziness. Avoid driving or participating in hazardous activities until you know how gabapentin will affect you. Dizziness or drowsiness can cause falls, accidents, or severe injuries.

Brand substitution. Brands of gabapentin may not be interchangeable.

Gabapentin — Gabapentin side effects
The most common side effects of gabapentin are dizziness, drowsiness (somnolence), swelling in the hands or feet (peripheral edema), and tiredness.

Other common gabapentin side effects may include:

- unsteadiness, poor coordination, or jerky movements
- nausea or vomiting
- headache
- trouble speaking
- blurred or double vision
- tremor
- signs of infection such as fever, chills, or sore throat

### Serious side effects

Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction to gabapentin: hives, difficult breathing, or swelling of your face, lips, tongue, or throat.

Seek medical treatment if you have a serious drug reaction that can affect many parts of your body. Symptoms may include skin rash, fever, swollen glands, muscle aches, severe weakness, unusual bruising, or yellowing of your skin or eyes.

Tell your doctor right away if you have new or sudden changes in mood or behavior, including new or worse depression or anxiety, panic attacks, trouble sleeping, or if you feel impulsive, irritable, agitated, hostile, aggressive, restless, more active or talkative, or have thoughts about suicide or hurting yourself, while taking this medicine and after stopping it.

Gabapentin can slow or stop your breathing, especially if you have recently used an opioid medicine or alcohol. A person caring for you should seek emergency medical attention if you have slow breathing with long pauses, blue-colored lips, or if you are hard to wake up.

Some side effects are more likely in children taking gabapentin. Call your doctor if the child has behavior changes, memory problems, trouble concentrating, or is restless, hostile, or aggressive.

Call your doctor at once if you have:

- drowsiness, dizziness, or weakness
- problems with balance or muscle movement
- increased seizures

This is not a complete list of side effects, and others may occur. Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects. You may report side effects to the FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.

Gabapentin — Before taking this medicine
You should not take gabapentin if you are allergic to it.

To make sure this medicine is safe for you, tell your doctor if you have ever had:

- breathing problems
- diabetes
- depression, a mood disorder, or suicidal thoughts or actions
- a history of alcohol use or drug addiction
- a seizure
- (for patients with restless legs syndrome) if you are a day sleeper or work a night shift
- kidney disease, or if you are on dialysis

Some people have thoughts about suicide while taking seizure medicine. Children taking gabapentin may have behavior changes. Stay alert to changes in your mood or symptoms. Your family or caregivers should also watch for sudden changes in your behavior.

### Pregnancy

It is not known if gabapentin will harm an unborn baby. Tell your doctor if you are pregnant or plan to become pregnant. Do not start or stop seizure medication during pregnancy without your doctor's advice, because having a seizure during pregnancy could harm both mother and baby. If you are pregnant, your name may be listed on a pregnancy registry to track the effects of gabapentin on the baby.

### Breastfeeding

Ask a doctor if it is safe to breastfeed while using gabapentin.

Gabapentin — How should I take gabapentin?
Take gabapentin exactly as prescribed by your doctor. Follow all directions on your prescription label and read all medication guides or instruction sheets. Your doctor may occasionally change your dose. Never take gabapentin in larger amounts, or for longer, than prescribed.

Your dose needs may change if you switch to a different brand, strength, or form of this medicine. Avoid medication errors by using only the medicine your doctor prescribes.

- Neurontin can be taken with or without food.
- Gralise should be taken with food, with the evening meal.
- Swallow Gralise and Horizant tablets whole. Do not crush, break, chew, or dissolve them.
- If you break a scored Neurontin tablet and take only half, take the other half at your next dose, and use it within 28 days.
- Measure liquid medicine with the supplied measuring device, not a kitchen spoon.

Doses are based on weight in children. Your child's dose may change if the child gains or loses weight.

Do not stop taking gabapentin suddenly. Stopping suddenly may cause increased seizures or withdrawal symptoms. Follow your doctor's instructions about tapering your dose, usually gradually over at least a week.

This medicine can affect the results of certain medical tests. Tell any doctor who treats you that you are using gabapentin. Your kidney function may need to be checked, and your dose may change based on the results.

Store tablets and capsules at room temperature away from moisture and heat. Store the liquid medicine in the refrigerator; do not freeze. Follow all storage instructions provided. Your pharmacist can provide more information about how to store this medicine.

Gabapentin — Gabapentin dosing information
Take gabapentin exactly as prescribed. Your dose may differ from the ranges shown here.

### Usual Gabapentin Dose for Epilepsy (immediate-release)

Patients 12 years and older Dose: starting dose gabapentin 300 mg 3 times a day, which may be titrated up to a maximum dose of 600 mg 3 times a day if needed.

Patients 3 to 11 years of age Dose: the starting dose range is 10 to 15 mg/kg/day, given in 3 divided doses. The recommended dose in patients 3 to 4 years of age is 40 mg/kg/day, and in patients 5 to 11 years of age is 25 to 35 mg/kg/day, given in 3 divided doses. The recommended maintenance dose is reached by upward titration over approximately 3 days.

Less than 3 years: Not recommended

Note:

- Leave no more than 12 hours between doses
- Dose should be adjusted in patients with reduced kidney function. Your doctor will determine your dose based on your kidney function.

Use: Adjunctive therapy in the treatment of partial onset seizures, with and without secondary generalization, in patients 3 years of age and older.

### Usual Adult Dose for Postherpetic Neuralgia (immediate-release)

Initial dose: gabapentin 300 mg orally on day one, 300 mg orally 2 times a day on day two, then 300 mg orally 3 times a day on day three, then titrate up as needed for pain relief

Maximum Gabapentin dose in 24 hours: 1800 mg per day (600 mg orally 3 times a day) Notes:

- Doses above 1,800 mg a day did not add benefit in studies.
- Dose should be modified in renal impairment.

### Usual Adult Dose for Postherpetic Neuralgia (extended release - Gralise)

Maintenance dose: Gralise should be titrated to 1800 mg orally once daily with the evening meal. Recommended titration schedule:

- Day 1: 300 mg orally with the evening meal
- Day 2: 600 mg orally with the evening meal
- Days 3 through 6: 900 mg orally with the evening meal
- Days 7 through 10: 1200 mg orally with the evening meal
- Days 11 through 14: 1500 mg orally with the evening meal
- Day 15: 1800 mg orally with the evening meal

Notes

- Gralise is not interchangeable with other gabapentin products because of differing pharmacokinetic profiles that affect the frequency of administration.
- Dose should be modified in renal impairment.

### Usual Adult Dose of Gabapentin enacarbil (Horizant) for Postherpetic Neuralgia

Initial dose: 600 mg orally once each morning on days 1 to 3 Maintenance dose: 600 mg orally 2 times a day (1200 mg/day total) from day 4 onward

Notes:

- Doses above 1200 mg a day did not add benefit and caused more side effects
- Take with food and swallow tablets whole; do not crush or chew
- Horizant is not interchangeable with other gabapentin products
- Dose should be modified in renal impairment

### Usual Adult Dose of Gabapentin enacarbil (Horizant) for Restless Legs Syndrome

Gabapentin enacarbil (Horizant) dose: 600 mg orally once daily with food at about 5 PM Use: For the treatment of moderate-to-severe primary Restless Legs Syndrome (RLS) in adults

Notes:

- A 1200 mg dose gave no added benefit and caused more side effects
- Dose should be modified in renal impairment.

Gabapentin — What happens if I miss a dose?
Take the medicine as soon as you can, but skip the missed dose if it is almost time for your next dose. Do not take two doses at one time.

If you take Horizant: skip the missed dose and take your next dose at the regular time. Do not use two doses at one time.

Gabapentin — What happens if I overdose?
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222. An overdose can be fatal.

Overdose symptoms may include slow breathing, double vision, tremor, slurred speech, drowsiness , change in your mental state, dizziness, tiredness, or diarrhea .

Gabapentin — What should I avoid while taking gabapentin?
Avoid driving or hazardous activities until you know how gabapentin will affect you. Dizziness or drowsiness can cause falls, accidents, or severe injuries.

Avoid taking antacids at the same time as gabapentin.

Do not drink alcohol. Dangerous side effects could occur.

Gabapentin — What other drugs will affect gabapentin?
Taking gabapentin with other medicines that make you drowsy or slow your breathing can cause dangerous side effects, including life-threatening breathing problems. Ask your doctor before using gabapentin with:

- opioid pain medicines such as hydrocodone, oxycodone, morphine, or buprenorphine
- sleeping pills such as zolpidem
- anxiety medicines such as lorazepam and other benzodiazepines
- muscle relaxers, sedatives, or other seizure medicines that cause drowsiness

Avoid drinking alcohol while taking gabapentin, because it can add to these effects.

Some medicines change the amount of gabapentin in your body:

- Antacids that contain aluminum or magnesium (such as Maalox) can reduce gabapentin absorption. Take gabapentin at least 2 hours after an antacid.
- Morphine can increase gabapentin levels, which may increase drowsiness and the risk of breathing problems. Your doctor may adjust your dose.

Gabapentin does not usually interact with other seizure medicines, such as phenytoin, carbamazepine, valproic acid, or phenobarbital, because it is not broken down by the liver. Your doctor can still check for interactions with your specific medicines.

Tell your doctor about all your current medicines, including prescription and over-the-counter medicines, vitamins, and herbal products. This list does not include all possible drug interactions.

Hydrochlorothiazide (HCTZ) — What is hydrochlorothiazide?
Hydrochlorothiazide is a thiazide diuretic , often called a water pill, used to treat high blood pressure and to reduce a build-up of fluid in the body ( edema ). It works by making the kidneys pass more salt and water out of the body, so you urinate more.

Hydrochlorothiazide is often shortened to HCTZ on prescriptions and medical records. It is available as a tablet, capsule, and liquid (suspension) taken by mouth and is usually taken once or twice a day.

Hydrochlorothiazide is available as:

- oral tablet (12.5 mg, 25 mg, and 50 mg)
- oral capsule (12.5 mg)
- powder for oral suspension (10 mg/mL)

Hydrochlorothiazide (HCTZ) brand names included HydroDIURIL tablets (discontinued), Esidrix (discontinued), and Microzide capsules (discontinued); now generic tablets and capsules are available. The brand name of hydrochlorothiazide oral suspension is Inzirqo.

Hydrochlorothiazide is also an ingredient in many combination blood pressure medicines, including with losartan, lisinopril, valsartan, and more.

Hydrochlorothiazide (HCTZ) — How does hydrochlorothiazide work?
Hydrochlorothiazide works by inhibiting the kidneys from reabsorbing sodium and chloride, so more salt and water leave the body in the urine, which helps reduce excess fluid buildup (edema). Exactly how thiazides lower blood pressure is not fully understood. The early effect is thought to come from a drop in blood volume. With longer-term use, blood volume returns towards normal while the blood vessels themselves offer less resistance to blood flow. Hydrochlorothiazide does not usually change blood pressure that is already normal.

Hydrochlorothiazide is a thiazide diuretic.

Hydrochlorothiazide (HCTZ) — Before taking this medicine
Do not take hydrochlorothiazide if you are unable to pass urine (anuria), or if you are allergic to hydrochlorothiazide or to other sulfonamide-derived medicines (sometimes called sulfa drugs).

Before you start HCTZ, tell your doctor if you have or have ever had:

- kidney disease or reduced kidney function
- liver disease, including cirrhosis
- diabetes
- gout or high uric acid levels
- high cholesterol or high triglycerides
- high calcium levels or a parathyroid gland disorder
- low potassium or low sodium levels
- lupus (systemic lupus erythematosus)
- glaucoma
- an allergy to sulfa medicines or to penicillin

Also tell your doctor if you are pregnant, planning to become pregnant, or breastfeeding. Hydrochlorothiazide may cause jaundice or bleeding problems in a newborn. It can also pass into breast milk and may cause serious side effects in a nursing baby, so you and your doctor should decide whether you will take this medicine or breastfeed.

Hydrochlorothiazide (HCTZ) — Warning and precautions
### Fluid and salt imbalance

Hydrochlorothiazide can lower the levels of potassium, sodium, magnesium and chloride in your blood. Low potassium (hypokalemia) is the most common of these and is more likely at higher doses, during rapid fluid loss, if you have severe cirrhosis, or after long-term use.. Your doctor will check your blood before you start and at intervals during treatment.

Contact your doctor if you have any of the following, which can be signs of a fluid or salt imbalance:

- dry mouth, thirst, or unusual weakness or tiredness
- drowsiness, restlessness or confusion
- muscle pain, cramps or muscle fatigue
- fast heartbeat
- passing much less urine than usual
- nausea or vomiting
- seizures

### Kidney and liver problems

HCTZ reduces the fluid volume in your body, which can lead to sudden kidney injury. The risk is higher if you have chronic kidney disease, heart failure, or are already low on fluid. If you have liver disease, small changes in fluid and salt balance can trigger a serious complication called hepatic coma. Your doctor will monitor your kidney function and watch for changes in alertness.

### Sudden vision changes need urgent care

Rarely, hydrochlorothiazide causes sudden short-sightedness (acute myopia) and a sharp rise in pressure inside the eye (acute angle-closure glaucoma), usually within hours to weeks of starting the medicine. Stop taking it and get medical help immediately if your vision drops suddenly or your eye hurts. Left untreated, this can cause permanent loss of vision. A history of allergy to sulfa medicines or penicillin may increase the risk.

### Blood sugar, cholesterol, uric acid and calcium

Hydrochlorothiazide can raise blood sugar, cholesterol, triglycerides, uric acid and calcium. Your diabetes medicine or insulin dose may need adjusting, and diabetes that was previously unnoticed can become apparent during treatment. The rise in uric acid is related to the dose and can cause gout or make existing gout worse. Because the medicine also affects calcium, it is stopped before parathyroid function tests, so tell any doctor arranging tests that you take it. Your doctor will check these levels from time to time.

### Skin cancer risk

Long-term use of hydrochlorothiazide is linked to a small increase in the risk of non-melanoma skin cancer, mainly squamous cell carcinoma. In a large safety study the increase was about 1 extra case per 16,000 people per year, rising to about 1 extra case per 6,700 people per year among white patients who had taken a total of 50,000 mg or more.

Protect your skin from the sun, wear protective clothing and use sunscreen, avoid sunlamps and tanning beds, and have regular skin checks. Tell your doctor about any new or changing skin growth, or a sore that does not heal.

### Lupus and allergic reactions

Thiazide diuretics have been reported to worsen lupus or bring it on. Sensitivity reactions can happen whether or not you have a history of allergies or asthma.

Hydrochlorothiazide (HCTZ) — Hydrochlorothiazide side effects
The most noticeable effect of hydrochlorothiazide is passing urine more often, particularly in the first few weeks. This is how the medicine works rather than a side effect, and it usually settles as your body adjusts.

### Serious side effects

Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction, such as hives, difficulty breathing, or swelling of your face, lips, tongue or throat.

Get emergency medical help if you have a severe skin reaction, such as a spreading red or purple rash with blistering and peeling, or skin that is painful to touch.

Stop taking hydrochlorothiazide and get medical help immediately if you have a sudden change in your vision or pain in your eye.

Call your doctor at once if you have:

- severe pain in your upper stomach that spreads to your back, with nausea and vomiting, which can be a sign of inflammation of the pancreas (pancreatitis)
- yellowing of your skin or the whites of your eyes (jaundice)
- fever, chills, sore throat, mouth sores or other signs of infection
- unusual bruising or bleeding, or pale skin and unusual tiredness, which can be signs of a blood problem
- shortness of breath, wheezing, or a cough with frothy mucus
- passing much less urine than usual, or swelling in your legs and ankles
- light-headedness or fainting, especially when standing up
- signs of dehydration or a salt imbalance, such as extreme thirst, dry mouth, confusion, muscle cramps, irregular heartbeat or unusual weakness

### Other side effects

Other hydrochlorothiazide side effects include:

- dizziness, a spinning sensation (vertigo), headache, restlessness, or tingling and numbness (paresthesia)
- weakness
- nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, constipation, stomach cramping, stomach irritation, or loss of appetite
- muscle spasm
- increased sensitivity to sunlight, causing sunburn more easily
- hair loss or thinning
- difficulty getting or keeping an erection
- temporary blurred vision, or objects looking yellow-tinged (xanthopsia)
- swelling and tenderness of the salivary glands
- raised blood sugar, sugar in the urine, or raised uric acid

If a side effect is moderate or severe, your doctor may lower your dose or stop the medicine. Do not stop taking HCTZ on your own without speaking to your doctor first.

This is not a complete list of side effects and others may occur. Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects. You may report side effects to the FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.

Hydrochlorothiazide (HCTZ) — Hydrochlorothiazide (HCTZ) dosage information
Your doctor will choose the smallest dose that controls your condition, and may adjust it over time. Follow your prescription label and do not change your dose yourself.

### HCTZ dose for high blood pressure (adults)

Tablets: The starting dose is hydrochlorothiazide 25 mg once a day, which may be increased to 50 mg a day, taken as a single dose or split into two doses. Doses above 50 mg a day are often associated with a marked drop in blood potassium. Most people do not need more than 50 mg a day when hydrochlorothiazide is taken alongside other blood pressure medicines

Capsules: The starting dose is a hydrochlorothiazide 12.5 mg capsule once a day, whether taken on its own or with other blood pressure medicines. Total daily doses above 50 mg are not recommended. Capsules are approved for high blood pressure only, not for fluid retention.

### HCTZ dose for fluid retention (adults)

Tablets : The usual dose is hydrochlorothiazide 25 mg to 100 mg a day, taken as a single dose or split into two doses. Many people with fluid retention respond to intermittent treatment, such as taking it every other day or on 3 to 5 days each week. Excessive response and undesirable electrolyte imbalance are less likely to occur with intermittent dosing.

### HCTZ dose for children for high blood pressure and fluid retention

The usual HCTZ dose in children, for both high blood pressure and fluid retention, is 1 mg to 2 mg per kilogram of body weight (0.5 mg to 1 mg per pound) a day, given as a single dose or split into two doses.

- Infants under 6 months: may be need doses of up to 3 mg per kilogram (1.5 mg per pound) a day, given as two divided doses up to the same maximum 37.5 mg daily limit
- Infants and children under 2 years: do not exceed 37.5 mg a day
- Children 2 to 12 years: do not exceed 100 mg a day

The dose is based on your child's weight and must be worked out by their doctor.

### Taking the oral suspension

If your child has been prescribed the liquid form (oral suspension), the following applies:

- Shake the bottle well before every dose.
- Measure each dose with the oral dosing syringe. Ask your pharmacist for a syringe and a bottle adapter if you do not have them.
- Never measure a dose with a household teaspoon or tablespoon, because these do not give an accurate amount.
- Store the made-up suspension at room temperature and do not freeze it.
- Throw away any unused suspension 30 days after it was made up. The pharmacist will write this date on the bottle.

The oral suspension can be taken with or without food.

### What happens if I miss a dose?

Take the medicine as soon as you can, but skip the missed dose if it is almost time for your next dose. Do not take two doses at one time.

### What happens if I overdose?

Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222.

Overdose symptoms may include nausea , dizziness , dry mouth, thirst, and muscle pain or weakness.

Hydrochlorothiazide (HCTZ) — What other drugs will affect hydrochlorothiazide?
Tell your doctor and pharmacist about all the medicines you take, including prescription and over-the-counter medicines, vitamins, and herbal products.

### Medicines that need particular care

- Lithium. Lithium and diuretics generally should not be taken together. Hydrochlorothiazide reduces how quickly the kidneys clear lithium, which carries a high risk of lithium toxicity.
- Cholestyramine and colestipol. These cholesterol-lowering resins bind hydrochlorothiazide in the gut and can reduce how much you absorb by up to 85 percent for cholestyramine and 43 percent for colestipol. The doses need to be separated. For the oral suspension, the label advises taking hydrochlorothiazide at least 4 hours before, or 4 to 6 hours after, the resin.
- NSAIDs such as ibuprofen (Advil, Motrin), naproxen (Aleve), diclofenac, meloxicam, indomethacin, celecoxib and aspirin. These can reduce the fluid-clearing and blood-pressure-lowering effects of hydrochlorothiazide, and your doctor will check whether the medicine is still working as intended.
- Insulin and oral diabetes medicines. Your diabetes medicine dose may need adjusting.
- Corticosteroids and ACTH. Taken with hydrochlorothiazide, these increase the loss of salts from the body, particularly potassium.
- Digoxin and other digitalis medicines. Low potassium caused by hydrochlorothiazide can make the heart more sensitive to the toxic effects of digitalis, including irregular heart rhythms.
- Other blood pressure medicines. The effects add together, which may be intended, but can also lower blood pressure more than expected.
- Alcohol, barbiturates and opioid pain medicines. These can make the drop in blood pressure on standing worse, causing dizziness or fainting.
- Muscle relaxants used during surgery (nondepolarizing muscle relaxants such as tubocurarine). You may respond more strongly to them, so tell your surgeon and anesthetist that you take hydrochlorothiazide.
- Medicines that raise blood pressure (pressor amines such as norepinephrine). Your response to them may be reduced, although this does not usually prevent their use.

This list is not complete. Other medicines may affect hydrochlorothiazide, and hydrochlorothiazide may affect other medicines.

Hydrocodone and Acetaminophen — What is hydrocodone + acetaminophen?
Hydrocodone and acetaminophen is a strong prescription combination pain medicine containing an opioid, hydrocodone, in addition to acetaminophen, which is used to manage moderate to moderately severe pain , when other pain treatments such as non-opioid pain medicines do not treat your pain well enough, or you cannot tolerate them.

An opioid pain medicine that can put you at risk for overdose and death. Even if you take your dose correctly as prescribed, you are at risk for opioid addiction, abuse, and misuse that can lead to death. Hydrocodone and acetaminophen is a Schedule II controlled substance .

All branded hydrocodone and acetaminophen ( Norco , Vicodin , Lortab , Hycet , Lorcet, Verdrocet , Xodol , Zamicet ) have been discontinued , but generic hydrocodone and acetaminophen tablets and oral solution is still available.

Hydrocodone and acetaminophen first gained FDA approval on May 3, 1982.

Hydrocodone and Acetaminophen — Important information about hydrocodone and acetaminophen
Get emergency help right away if you take too many hydrocodone and acetaminophen tablets or oral solution (overdose). When you first start taking hydrocodone and acetaminophen, when your dose is changed, or if you take too much (overdose), serious or life-threatening breathing problems that can lead to death may occur.

Never give anyone else your hydrocodone and acetaminophen . They could die from taking them. Store hydrocodone and acetaminophen away from children and in a safe place to prevent stealing or abuse. Selling or giving away hydrocodone and acetaminophen products is against the law.

Hydrocodone and Acetaminophen — Side Effects
### Common Side Effects

The possible side effects of hydrocodone and acetaminophen are constipation, nausea, sleepiness, vomiting, tiredness, headache, dizziness, and abdominal pain. Call your healthcare provider if you have any of these symptoms and they are severe.

Get emergency medical help if you have:

- Trouble breathing
- Shortness of breath
- Fast heartbeat
- Chest pain
- Swelling of your face, tongue, or throat
- Extreme drowsiness
- Light-headedness when changing positions
- Feeling faint
- Agitation
- High body temperature
- Trouble walking
- Stiff muscles
- Mental changes such as confusion.

These are not all the possible side effects of hydrocodone and acetaminophen. Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects. You may report side effects to the FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.

Hydrocodone and Acetaminophen — Before Taking
Do not take hydrocodone and acetaminophen if you have:

- Severe asthma, trouble breathing, or other lung problems
- A bowel blockage or a narrowing of the stomach or intestines
- Signs of an allergy, such as a rash
- Are allergic to any of its ingredients.

Before taking hydrocodone and acetaminophen, tell your healthcare provider if you have a history of:

- Head injury, seizures
- Liver, kidney, thyroid problems
- Problems urinating
- Pancreas or gallbladder problems
- Abuse of street or prescription drugs, alcohol addiction, or mental health problems.

Tell your healthcare provider if you are:

- Pregnant or planning to become pregnant. Prolonged use of hydrocodone and acetaminophen during pregnancy can cause withdrawal symptoms in your newborn baby that could be life-threatening if not recognized and treated
- Breastfeeding. Hydrocodone and acetaminophen pass into breast milk and may harm your baby. Carefully observe infants for increased sleepiness (more than usual), breathing difficulties, or limpness. Seek immediate medical care if you notice these signs.
- Taking prescription or over-the-counter medicines, vitamins, or herbal supplements. Taking hydrocodone and acetaminophen with certain other medicines can cause serious side effects that could lead to death.

Hydrocodone and Acetaminophen — How Do I Take Hydrocodone and Acetaminophen
When taking hydrocodone and acetaminophen:

- Do not change your dose . Take hydrocodone and acetaminophen exactly as prescribed by your healthcare provider.
- Take your prescribed dose every four to six hours at the same time every day. Do not take more than your prescribed dose. If you miss a dose, take your next dose at your usual time.
- If taking the oral solution, always use a graduated oral syringe or measuring cup to correctly measure your dose. Never use a household teaspoon or tablespoon to measure your medicine.
- Call your healthcare provider if the dose you are taking does not control your pain, if your pain gets worse, if you feel more sensitive to pain, or if you have new pain. Do not take more medicine without first talking to your healthcare provider.

If you have been taking hydrocodone and acetaminophen regularly, do not stop taking it without talking to your healthcare provider.

After you stop taking hydrocodone and acetaminophen, flush any unused tablets or oral solution down the toilet .

Hydrocodone and Acetaminophen — What Should I Avoid While Taking Hydrocodone and Acetaminophen
While taking hydrocodone and acetaminophen, DO NOT:

- Drive or operate heavy machinery until you know how hydrocodone and acetaminophen affects you. Hydrocodone and acetaminophen can make you sleepy, dizzy, or lightheaded.
- Drink alcohol or use prescription or over-the-counter medicines that contain alcohol . Using products containing alcohol during treatment with hydrocodone and acetaminophen may cause you to overdose and die.

Hydrocodone and Acetaminophen — Dosing Information
### Adult Dose of Hydrocodone And Acetaminophen for Moderate to Severe Pain

#### Hydrocodone 2.5 mg-acetaminophen 325 mg per tablet, Hydrocodone 5 mg-acetaminophen 325 mg per tablet

- Initial dose: 1 to 2 tablets every 4 to 6 hours as needed for pain
- Maximum dose: 8 tablets/24 hours

#### Hydrocodone 7.5 mg-acetaminophen 325 mg, Hydrocodone 10 mg-acetaminophen 325 mg per tablet

- Initial dose: 1 tablet every 4 to 6 hours as needed for pain
- Maximum dose: 6 tablets/24 hours

#### Hydrocodone 7.5 mg-acetaminophen 325 mg per 15 mL oral solution

- Initial dose: 15 mL every 4 to 6 hours as needed for pain
- Maximum dose: 90 mL/24 hours

#### Hydrocodone 10 mg-acetaminophen 300 mg per 15 mL oral solution

- Initial dose: 11.25 mL every 4 to 6 hours as needed for pain
- Maximum dose: 67.5 mL/24 hours

#### Comments:

- Doses should be individually titrated to provide adequate analgesia while minimizing adverse reactions.
- Because of the risks of addiction, abuse, and misuse, the lowest effective dose for the shortest duration consistent with individual patient treatment goals should be used.
- Monitor patients closely for respiratory depression within the first 24 to 72 hours of initiating therapy and following any increase in dose.

Hydrocodone and Acetaminophen — What happens if I overdose?
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222. An overdose of acetaminophen and hydrocodone can be fatal.

Acute overdosage with hydrocodone and acetaminophen can cause respiratory depression, sleepiness progressing to near-unconsciousness or coma, floppy muscles, cold and clammy skin, constricted pupils, and, in some cases, pulmonary edema, slow heartbeat, low blood pressure, partial or complete airway obstruction, unusual snoring, and death. If the patient who has overdosed becomes very low in oxygen, pupils may become very dilated instead of constricted.

Your doctor may recommend that you get naloxone (a medicine to reverse an opioid overdose) and keep it with you at all times. A person caring for you can give the naloxone if you stop breathing or don't wake up. Your caregiver must still get emergency medical help and may need to perform CPR (cardiopulmonary resuscitation) on you while waiting for help to arrive.

Anyone can buy naloxone from a pharmacy or local health department. Make sure any person caring for you knows where you keep naloxone and how to use it.

The first signs of an acetaminophen overdose include loss of appetite, nausea , vomiting , stomach pain, sweating, and confusion or weakness. Later symptoms may include pain in your upper stomach, dark urine, and yellowing of your skin or the whites of your eyes.

Hydrocodone and Acetaminophen — What other drugs will affect hydrocodone and acetaminophen?
You may have breathing problems or withdrawal symptoms if you start or stop taking certain other medicines. Tell your doctor if you also use an antibiotic , antifungal medication, heart or blood pressure medication, seizure medication, or medicine to treat HIV or hepatitis C.

Opioid medication can interact with many other drugs and cause dangerous side effects or death. Be sure your doctor knows if you also use:

- cold or allergy medicines, bronchodilator asthma/ COPD medication, or a diuretic ("water pill");
- medicines for motion sickness , irritable bowel syndrome , or overactive bladder ;
- other opioids - opioid pain medicine or prescription cough medicine;
- a sedative like Valium - diazepam , alprazolam , lorazepam , Xanax , Klonopin , Versed , and others;
- drugs that make you sleepy or slow your breathing - a sleeping pill, muscle relaxer, medicine to treat mood disorders or mental illness;
- drugs that affect serotonin levels in your body - a stimulant, or medicine for depression , Parkinson's disease , migraine headaches, serious infections, or nausea and vomiting .

This list is not complete. Other drugs may interact with acetaminophen and hydrocodone, including prescription and over-the-counter medicines, vitamins , and herbal products . Not all possible interactions are listed here. To check for interactions with hydrocodone and acetaminophen, click the link below.

Remember, keep this and all other medicines out of the reach of children, never share your medicines with others, and use acetaminophen or hydrocodone only for the indication prescribed.

Hydroxyzine — What is hydroxyzine?
Hydroxyzine is a prescription antihistamine used to treat anxiety and tension, itching from allergic skin conditions, and sedation before and after general anesthesia.

It comes in three oral forms:

- Hydroxyzine hydrochloride (HCl) tablets in 10 mg, 25 mg, and 50 mg strengths
- Hydroxyzine hydrochloride oral solution (a syrup) containing 10 mg in each 5 mL
- Hydroxyzine pamoate capsules in 25 mg

There is also a hydroxyzine hydrochloride injection. It is given into a muscle by a healthcare professional and is never given into a vein or under the skin.

Hydroxyzine is not chemically related to benzodiazepines, phenothiazines, reserpine, or meprobamate.

Hydroxyzine — How does hydroxyzine work?
Hydroxyzine is thought to work by quieting activity in certain deeper regions of the brain, below the outer layer known as the cortex. It is not a cortical depressant, meaning it does not work by slowing down the outer layer of the brain the way some other sedatives do.

Hydroxyzine also blocks histamine, a chemical your body releases during an allergic reaction. This is what relieves itching.

Laboratory and clinical studies have also shown that hydroxyzine relaxes skeletal muscle, widens the airways (bronchodilation), and reduces pain. These are effects that have been observed rather than uses the medicine is approved for.

Hydroxyzine has also been shown to reduce nausea and vomiting. This is an approved use for the injection, but not for the forms taken by mouth.

Hydroxyzine — How do I take hydroxyzine?
Take hydroxyzine exactly as your doctor prescribes. Follow all directions on your prescription label. Do not take more or less than prescribed, and do not take it for longer than your doctor tells you to.

Your doctor may adjust your dose depending on how well you respond to treatment.

If you are taking the oral solution, measure your dose with the dosing syringe provided, a dose-measuring spoon, or a medicine cup. Do not use a kitchen spoon, because it will not give an accurate dose. Ask your pharmacist for a measuring device if you do not have one.

Call your doctor if your anxiety does not improve or if it gets worse.

Hydroxyzine — What is the usual dosage of hydroxyzine?
The dose depends on what you are being treated for. The same doses apply to hydroxyzine hydrochloride tablets, hydroxyzine hydrochloride oral solution, and hydroxyzine pamoate capsules.

### Dosage for anxiety and tension

- Adults: 50 to 100 mg four times a day
- Children under 6 years: 50 mg a day, split into smaller doses
- Children 6 years and over: 50 to 100 mg a day, split into smaller doses

### Dosage for itching

- Adults: 25 mg three or four times a day
- Children under 6 years: 50 mg a day, split into smaller doses
- Children 6 years and over: 50 to 100 mg a day, split into smaller doses

### Dosage for sedation before and after surgery

- Adults: 50 to 100 mg
- Children: 0.6 mg for each kilogram of body weight

If treatment is started with an injection into a muscle, later doses can be given by mouth.

### Dosage in older adults

Doctors usually start older adults on a low dose. Sedating medicines can cause confusion and too much sedation in older people, so your doctor will watch you closely. Kidney, liver, and heart function often decline with age, and how much hydroxyzine leaves the body through the kidneys has not been established.

Hydroxyzine — What are the side effects of hydroxyzine?
The most common side effects of hydroxyzine are drowsiness, dry mouth, and headache. These are usually mild and do not last long.

A skin rash is different. If you develop a rash while taking hydroxyzine, stop taking it and call your doctor, because a rash can be the first sign of a serious reaction.

### Common side effects

Common hydroxyzine side effects include dry mouth, headache, and drowsiness. The drowsiness is usually temporary and may go away after a few days of treatment or when the dose is lowered.

### Serious side effects

Stop taking hydroxyzine and call your doctor right away if you develop a skin rash, if a skin condition you are treating gets worse, or if you have any other sign of an allergic reaction.

In rare cases, hydroxyzine can cause a serious skin reaction called acute generalized exanthematous pustulosis (AGEP). Signs include fever together with many small, shallow, pus-filled spots appearing across large areas of swollen, red skin. If you have had AGEP or another allergic reaction to hydroxyzine, you should also avoid cetirizine and levocetirizine, because a similar reaction can happen with those medicines.

Hydroxyzine has also been linked to a heart rhythm problem called QT prolongation, which can lead to a serious irregular heartbeat known as Torsade de Pointes.

Other reported side effects include:

- tremor or seizures (convulsions), which are rare and have usually happened at doses much higher than recommended
- hallucinations (seeing or hearing things that are not there)
- skin reactions including rash, hives, itching, and fixed drug eruptions, which are rashes that come back in the same spot each time the medicine is taken
- allergic reaction

Serious breathing problems have not been reported at recommended doses.

If you are given hydroxyzine by injection, tell your healthcare provider straight away about severe pain, swelling, or skin changes where the injection was given. In rare cases, the injection has caused serious damage to the surrounding tissue, including tissue death (necrosis) and gangrene, which has needed surgery to treat.

This is not a complete list of side effects, and others may happen. Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects. You may report side effects to the FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.

Hydroxyzine — Who should not take hydroxyzine?
Do not take hydroxyzine if you:

- are allergic to hydroxyzine or to any ingredient in the medicine
- are allergic to cetirizine or levocetirizine
- have a prolonged QT interval, a heart rhythm finding seen on an electrocardiogram (ECG)
- are in early pregnancy

In animal studies, hydroxyzine caused birth defects in rats and mice at doses well above those used in people. There is not enough information in humans to know whether hydroxyzine is safe in early pregnancy, so it should not be used at that time.

Hydroxyzine — What should I avoid while taking hydroxyzine?
