Others take it every day for months or years for an ongoing condition such as rheumatoid arthritis or ulcerative colitis.

If you take prednisone long term, your doctor will settle you on a maintenance dose. This is the lowest daily dose that still keeps your condition under control, and it is reached by lowering the dose in small steps rather than all at once. Your maintenance dose is not fixed. It may need to go up for a time if your condition flares, or if you have surgery, a fever, an infection, or another serious illness, and it may come down again afterwards. Do not adjust it yourself.

### How do you stop taking prednisone safely?

If you have taken prednisone for more than a few days, your doctor will taper the dose rather than stopping it all at once. While you are taking a steroid, your adrenal glands slow down their own hormone production, and they need time to start up again. Stopping suddenly can cause extreme tiredness, weakness, body aches, upset stomach, weight loss, a craving for salt, and changes in skin color. Call your doctor if you feel unwell while your dose is being lowered or after you finish.

### Prednisone formulations

- Prednisone 1 mg tablets
- Prednisone 2.5 mg tablets
- Prednisone 5 mg tablets
- Prednisone 10 mg tablets
- Prednisone 20 mg tablets
- Prednisone 50 mg tablets
- Prednisone Oral Solution 5 mg per 5 mL
- Prednisone Intensol™ Oral Solution (Concentrate) 5 mg per mL
- Prednisone delayed-release tablets (Rayos) 1 mg, 2 mg, and 5 mg

Prednisone — What happens if I miss a dose?
Take the missed dose as soon as you remember. Skip it if it is almost time for your next dose. Do not take extra medicine to make up a missed dose. If you take prednisone once every other day, or you are following a taper, call your doctor or pharmacist for advice rather than guessing.

Prednisone — What happens if I overdose?
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222.

A single large dose of prednisone is not likely to cause life-threatening symptoms. The bigger risk comes from taking high doses over a long time, which can cause thinning skin, easy bruising, changes in the shape or location of body fat, acne, extra facial hair, irregular periods, and loss of interest in sex.

Prednisone — What should I avoid while taking prednisone?
- Avoid being near people who are sick or have an infection. Call your doctor if you are exposed to chickenpox or measles.
- Do not have a live vaccine, such as MMR, chickenpox, rotavirus, yellow fever, oral typhoid, or the nasal flu vaccine, without your doctor's approval. Avoid close contact with someone who has just had an oral or nasal live vaccine.
- Limit alcohol. Both alcohol and prednisone irritate the stomach lining, so together they raise the risk of stomach bleeding.
- Be careful with NSAIDs such as ibuprofen, naproxen, and aspirin, since taking them with prednisone raises the risk of stomach bleeding.
- Your doctor may ask you to cut back on salt, since prednisone makes your body hold onto fluid.

Prednisone — What other drugs will affect prednisone?
Many medicines interact with prednisone. Some change how much prednisone is in your body, and others add to its side effects. Tell your doctor about every medicine you take, including anything you start or stop during treatment. Be especially careful with:

- medicines that raise prednisone levels, such as ketoconazole, itraconazole, posaconazole, voriconazole, clarithromycin, and some HIV and hepatitis C medicines;
- medicines that lower prednisone levels and can make it work less well, such as rifampin, rifabutin, rifapentine, carbamazepine, phenytoin, phenobarbital, primidone and St. John's wort;
- NSAIDs and aspirin, which raise the risk of stomach ulcers and bleeding;
- blood thinners such as warfarin, since prednisone can change how your blood clots and you may need extra monitoring;
- insulin and diabetes tablets, since prednisone raises blood sugar and your doses may need to go up;
- diuretics (water pills) and amphotericin B, which can lower potassium further;
- digoxin, which is more likely to cause problems if your potassium is low;
- live vaccines, which should usually be avoided;
- cyclosporine, which interacts in both directions and raises the risk of seizures; and
- birth control pills and hormone therapy.

This list is not complete. Many other prescription medicines, over-the-counter products, vitamins and herbal products can interact with prednisone. Give a list of all your medicines to any healthcare provider who treats you.

Rosuvastatin — What is rosuvastatin?
Rosuvastatin (Crestor) is a prescription medicine used to treat high cholesterol and to reduce the risk of major cardiovascular events such as heart attack and stroke.

Rosuvastatin belongs to a class of medicines called statins. It works by blocking HMG-CoA reductase, the rate-limiting enzyme the liver uses to make cholesterol. With cholesterol synthesis reduced, the liver increases its LDL receptors and clears more LDL, or "bad" cholesterol, from the blood. LDL and total cholesterol levels fall, with the maximum reduction usually reached by four weeks.

Rosuvastatin comes as a tablet. It was first approved by the US Food and Drug Administration (FDA) on August 12, 2003, under the brand name Crestor. Many generic versions of rosuvastatin tablets have since been approved.

Rosuvastatin — What are the side effects of rosuvastatin?
### Common side effects

The most common side effects may include headache, nausea, muscle aches and pains, weakness, and constipation.

### Serious side effects

Rosuvastatin may cause serious side effects, including:

- Muscle pain, tenderness and weakness (myopathy). Muscle problems, including muscle breakdown, can be serious in some people and rarely cause kidney damage that can lead to death. Tell your doctor right away if: you have unexplained muscle pain, tenderness, or weakness, especially if you have a fever or feel more tired than usual, while you take this medicine. you have muscle problems that do not go away even after your doctor has told you to stop taking this medicine. Your doctor may do further tests to diagnose the cause of your muscle problems. Your chances of getting muscle problems are higher if you: are taking certain other medicines while you take rosuvastatin are 65 years of age or older are of Asian descent have thyroid problems (hypothyroidism) that are not controlled have kidney problems are taking higher doses of this medicine
- Liver problems. Your doctor may do blood tests to check your liver before you start taking this medicine and if you have symptoms of liver problems while you are taking it. Call your doctor right away if you have any of the following symptoms of liver problems: feel unusually tired or weak loss of appetite upper belly pain dark urine yellowing of your skin or the whites of your eyes
- Protein and blood in the urine. Rosuvastatin may cause you to have protein and blood in your urine. If you develop protein or blood in your urine, your doctor may decrease your dose.
- Increase in blood sugar (glucose) levels. Rosuvastatin may cause an increase in your blood sugar levels.

Additional side effects that have been reported with rosuvastatin include abdominal pain, dizziness, memory loss and confusion.

Tell your doctor if you have any side effect that bothers you or that does not go away.

These are not all the possible side effects. For more information, ask your doctor or pharmacist.

Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects. You may report side effects to the FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.

Rosuvastatin — Who should not take rosuvastatin?
Do not take rosuvastatin if you:

- are allergic to rosuvastatin or any of the ingredients in your rosuvastatin tablets. See below for a complete list of ingredients.
- have liver problems.

Rosuvastatin pregnancy and breastfeeding warnings (more detail)

Rosuvastatin — How should I take rosuvastatin?
- Take rosuvastatin exactly as your doctor tells you to.
- Take rosuvastatin by mouth once daily. Swallow the tablet whole.
- Rosuvastatin can be taken at any time of day, with or without food.
- Do not change your dose or stop this medicine without talking to your doctor, even if you are feeling well.
- Your doctor may do blood tests to check your cholesterol levels before and during your treatment with rosuvastatin. Your doctor may change your dose if needed.
- Your doctor may start you on a cholesterol-lowering diet before giving you rosuvastatin. Stay on this diet when you take this medicine.
- If you take an antacid that contains a combination of aluminum and magnesium hydroxide, take rosuvastatin at least 2 hours before you take the antacid.

Rosuvastatin — What happens if I miss a dose?
If you miss a dose of rosuvastatin, take your next dose at your normal scheduled time. Do not take an extra dose.

Rosuvastatin — What happens if I overdose?
In case of an overdose, get medical help or contact a live Poison Center expert right away at 1-800-222-1222. Advice is also available online at poisonhelp.org.

Rosuvastatin — Dosing information
- Dose range: 5 mg to 40 mg once daily. The dose depends on the reason you are taking it, your LDL cholesterol level, and your individual risk of cardiovascular events.
- People of Asian descent: the recommended starting dose is 5 mg once daily.
- Severe kidney impairment (not on hemodialysis): the recommended starting dose is 5 mg once daily, and the dose should not exceed 10 mg once daily.
- Children with homozygous familial hypercholesterolemia: 20 mg once daily for those 7 years of age and older.
- Children with heterozygous familial hypercholesterolemia: 5 mg to 10 mg once daily for those 8 years to less than 10 years of age, and 5 mg to 20 mg once daily for those 10 years of age and older.
- Some medicines require a lower rosuvastatin dose, and a few should not be taken with rosuvastatin at all. See the detailed dosage information for the full list.

Rosuvastatin — What other drugs will affect rosuvastatin?
Tell your doctor about all the medicines you take, including prescription and over-the-counter medicines, vitamins, and herbal supplements. Also tell the doctor who prescribes rosuvastatin if another doctor increases the dose of another medicine you are taking. Rosuvastatin may affect the way other medicines work, and other medicines may affect how rosuvastatin works. Especially tell your doctor if you take:

- coumarin anticoagulants (medicines that prevent blood clots, such as warfarin)
- antacids (medicines you take for heartburn that contain aluminum and magnesium hydroxide)

Taking rosuvastatin with certain medicines may increase the risk of muscle problems. Especially tell your doctor if you take:

- cyclosporine (a medicine for your immune system)
- teriflunomide (a medicine used to treat relapsing remitting multiple sclerosis)
- enasidenib (a medicine used to treat acute myeloid leukemia)
- capmatinib (a medicine for the treatment of non-small cell lung cancer)
- fostamatinib (a medicine used to treat low platelet counts)
- febuxostat (a medicine used to treat and prevent high blood levels of uric acid)
- gemfibrozil (a fibric acid medicine for lowering cholesterol)
- tafamidis [used to treat cardiomyopathy (enlarged and thickened heart muscle)]
- anti-viral medicines including certain HIV or hepatitis C virus drugs such as: lopinavir, ritonavir, fosamprenavir, tipranavir, atazanavir, simeprevir combination of sofosbuvir/velpatasvir/voxilaprevir dasabuvir/ombitasvir/paritaprevir/ritonavir elbasvir/grazoprevir sofosbuvir/velpatasvir glecaprevir/pibrentasvir and all other combinations with ledipasvir including ledipasvir/sofosbuvir
- darolutamide (a medicine for the treatment of prostate cancer)
- regorafenib (a medicine used to treat cancer of the colon and rectum)
- fibric acid derivatives (such as fenofibrate)
- ticagrelor (helps reduce the chance of blood clot formation that can block a blood vessel)
- niacin or nicotinic acid
- colchicine (a medicine used to treat gout)
- belumosudil (used to treat chronic graft-versus-host disease)
- momelotinib (used to treat myelofibrosis, a rare form of blood cancer)

Ask your doctor or pharmacist for a list of these medicines if you are not sure. Know all of the medicines you take. Keep a list of them to show your doctor and pharmacist when you get new medicine.

Rosuvastatin — Pregnancy and breastfeeding
Tell your doctor if you are pregnant or think you may be pregnant, or are planning to become pregnant. Rosuvastatin may harm your unborn baby. If you become pregnant while taking rosuvastatin, call your doctor right away to discuss your treatment.

Tell your doctor if you are breastfeeding or plan to breastfeed. Medicines like rosuvastatin can pass into your breast milk and may harm your baby. Talk to your doctor about the best way to feed your baby if you take this medicine. Do not breastfeed while taking rosuvastatin.

Rosuvastatin — Storage
- Store rosuvastatin at room temperature, between 68°F and 77°F (20°C and 25°C).
- Store in a dry place.
- Safely throw away medicine that is out of date or no longer needed.
- Keep this and all medicines out of the reach of children.

Rosuvastatin — What are the ingredients in rosuvastatin?
Active ingredient: rosuvastatin calcium

Inactive ingredients:

Crestor tablets: microcrystalline cellulose NF, lactose monohydrate NF, tribasic calcium phosphate NF, crospovidone NF, magnesium stearate NF, hypromellose NF, triacetin NF, titanium dioxide USP, yellow ferric oxide, and red ferric oxide NF.

Generic versions of rosuvastatin tablets may contain different inactive ingredients from those listed above . Check the product label for your particular formulation for a complete list of ingredients.

Semaglutide — What is semaglutide?
Semaglutide ( Ozempic , Wegovy , Rybelsus ) is a prescription GLP-1 medication used for weight loss and weight management, type 2 diabetes treatment, or to reduce the risk of chronic kidney disease and major cardiovascular events in certain patients with type 2 diabetes. Semaglutide (Wegovy) is also used to treat metabolic dysfunction-associated steatohepatitis (MASH), without cirrhosis of the liver.

Semaglutide injections are self-administered once weekly as an injection under the skin, and semaglutide pills are taken once daily.

Semaglutide works by reducing appetite, delaying gastric emptying, increasing insulin release, and lowering the amount of glucagon released. Semaglutide is a GLP-1 receptor agonist that mimics the behavior of the GLP-1 hormone, which is naturally released after eating.

Semaglutide brand names are Ozempic, Rybelsus, and Wegovy, all made by Novo Nordisk. There is no generic semaglutide available. Semaglutide FDA approval was first granted on December 5, 2017, for the brand Ozempic.

Each semaglutide brand or formulation is FDA-approved for specific conditions and patient populations.

Semaglutide — How does semaglutide work?
Semaglutide works by:

- Stimulates insulin production in the pancreas
- Reduces sugar production in the liver
- Slows down digestion, so food stays in the stomach longer
- Decreases appetite and food intake

These combined actions help you feel full longer, eat less, and maintain better control over blood sugar levels. As a result, semaglutide supports weight loss , improves blood sugar management , and helps reduce the risk of cardiovascular events .

Semaglutide's mechanism of action mimics a natural hormone in the body called GLP-1 (glucagon-like peptide-1), which is released after eating to help regulate appetite and blood sugar levels.

The way semaglutide works for kidney-related risk reduction is not fully understood.

Semaglutide belongs to the drug class called GLP-1 receptor agonists.

Semaglutide — Semaglutide side effects
### Common semaglutide side effects

Common semaglutide side effects may include upset stomach , heartburn , burping , gas, bloating, nausea, vomiting, stomach pain, loss of appetite, diarrhea, constipation, stomach flu symptoms, low blood sugar (in people with type 2 diabetes), headache, dizziness, tiredness, hair loss, and changes in skin sensations.

Stomach or gastrointestinal side effects are common, but they tend to be mild and clear up in a few weeks and usually will not interfere with long-term treatment. Stomach side effects can be more common with higher doses.

#### Tips for managing stomach side effects

Following these tips may help you manage stomach semaglutide side effects:

- Eat more slowly
- Eat smaller meals
- Choose more bland, low-fat foods (like crackers, toast, and rice)
- Avoid eating greasy, fried foods or sugar treats
- Eat foods that contain water (like soup or gelatin)
- Do not lie down right after eating
- Drink clear or ice-cold liquids
- If possible, go outside for fresh air if you feel sick to your stomach
- Ryebelsus is not recommended in patients with severe gastroparesis.

#### Tips for managing low blood sugar levels (hypoglycemia) side effects

- Test blood sugar levels regularly if you are also taking diabetic medicines, including insulin or sulphonylureas (e.g., glibenclamide/glyburide, glipizide, and gliclazide) and when you increase your semaglutide dose.
- Your prescriber might reduce your dose of insulin or sulphonylureas if needed to reduce the risk of hypoglycemia
- Symptoms of low blood sugar levels include:
- Treatment for hypoglycemia
- Severe hypoglycemia may require a glucagon injection.

### Serious semaglutide side effects

Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction: hives , itching, dizziness, fast heartbeats, difficulty breathing, swelling of your face, lips, tongue, or throat.

Call your doctor at once if you have:

- vision changes;
- unusual mood changes, thoughts about hurting yourself;
- pounding heartbeats or fluttering in your chest;
- a light-headed feeling, like you might pass out;
- signs of a thyroid tumor - swelling or a lump in your neck, trouble swallowing, a hoarse voice, feeling short of breath;
- symptoms of pancreatitis - severe pain in your upper stomach spreading to your back, nausea with or without vomiting, fast heart rate ;
- gallbladder problems - upper stomach pain, fever, clay-colored stools, jaundice (yellowing of the skin or eyes);
- low blood sugar - headache , hunger, weakness, sweating, confusion, irritability, dizziness, fast heart rate, or feeling jittery;
- kidney problems - swelling, urinating less, feeling tired or short of breath; or
- stomach flu symptoms - stomach cramps, vomiting, loss of appetite, diarrhea (may be watery or bloody); or severe stomach problems like severe constipation, fecal impaction, or intestinal obstruction.

This is not a complete list of side effects, and others may occur. Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects. You may report side effects to the FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.

Semaglutide — Warnings
Semaglutide has a boxed warning for the risk of thyroid C-cell tumors. A boxed warning is the strongest safety-related warning issued by the FDA.  Semaglutide may cause serious side effects, including possible thyroid tumors and thyroid cancer .

Call your doctor at once if you have signs of a thyroid tumor, such as swelling or a lump in your neck, trouble swallowing, a hoarse voice, or shortness of breath.

You should not use semaglutide if you have multiple endocrine neoplasia type 2 (tumors in your glands) or a personal or family history of medullary thyroid cancer .

Semaglutide also has warnings for pancreatitis, diabetic retinopathy complications, hypoglycemia that can be severe, acute kidney injury, acute gallbladder disease, hypersensitivity reactions, and pulmonary aspiration during general anesthesia or deep sedation. For Wegovy, there are also warnings for a heart rate increase and suicidal behaviour or ideation. For Rybelsus, there is also a warning for severe gastrointestinal side effects.

Semaglutide — Before taking this medicine
You should not use semaglutide if you are allergic to it, or if you have:

- multiple endocrine neoplasia type 2 (tumors in your glands);
- a personal or family history of medullary thyroid carcinoma (a type of thyroid cancer); or
- diabetic ketoacidosis (call your doctor for treatment).

Tell your doctor if you have ever had:

- a stomach or intestinal disorder;
- pancreatitis ;
- are planning surgery or other procedure that uses anesthesia or deep sedation;
- kidney disease; or
- eye problems caused by diabetes ( retinopathy ).

In animal studies, semaglutide caused thyroid tumors or thyroid cancer. It is not known whether these effects would occur in people. Ask your doctor about your risk.

### Pregnancy

Men and women should stop using semaglutide at least 2 months before they plan to get pregnant. Ask your doctor for a safer medicine to use during this time. Controlling diabetes is very important during pregnancy, as is gaining the right amount of weight. Even if you are overweight, losing weight during pregnancy could harm the unborn baby.

There is a pregnancy exposure registry for women who use Wegovy during pregnancy. The purpose of this registry is to collect information about the health of you and your baby. Talk to your healthcare provider about how you can take part in this registry or contact Novo Nordisk at 1-877-390-2760 or www.wegovypregnancyregistry.com.

### Breastfeeding

Ask a doctor if it is safe to breastfeed while using Ozempic or Wegovy injections. Breastfeeding is not recommended during treatment with Wegovy tablets.

You should not breastfeed while using Rybelsus.

Semaglutide — How should I use or take semaglutide?
### Semaglutide injection directions

Semaglutide injections ( Ozempic and Wegovy) are injected under the skin, usually once per week at any time of the day, with or without food. Use an injection on the same day each week.

Read and follow all instructions you have been given. Ask your doctor or pharmacist if you need help.

Prepare an injection only when you are ready to give it. Call your pharmacist if the medicine looks cloudy, has changed colors, or has particles in it.

Your healthcare provider will show you where to inject semaglutide. Do not inject into the same place twice in a row. If you are visually impaired, the Wegovy FlexTouch pen is not recommended for self-injection.

If you choose a different weekly injection day, start your new schedule after at least 2 days have passed since the last injection you gave.

Do not use different brands of semaglutide at the same time.

### Semaglutide tablets directions

Semaglutide oral tablets (Ozempic, Wegovy, Rybelsus) are taken once a day at least 30 minutes before the first food, beverage, or other oral medications of the day with no more than 4 ounces of plain water only. After 30 minutes of taking the tablet, you may eat, drink, or take other oral medicines. Swallow tablets whole. Do not split, crush, or chew tablets.

### General semaglutide use

Blood sugar can be affected by stress , illness, surgery, exercise, alcohol use, or skipping meals.

Low blood sugar (hypoglycemia) can make you feel very hungry, dizzy, irritable, or shaky. To quickly treat hypoglycemia , eat or drink hard candy, crackers, raisins, fruit juice, or non-diet soda. Your doctor may prescribe glucagon injections in case of severe hypoglycemia.

Tell your doctor if you have frequent symptoms of high blood sugar (hyperglycemia), such as increased thirst or urination. Ask your doctor before changing your dose or medication schedule.

Your treatment may also include diet, exercise, weight control, medical tests, and special medical care.

You may get dehydrated during prolonged illness. Call your doctor if you are sick with vomiting or diarrhea , or if you eat or drink less than usual.

Never share an injection pen, even if you changed the needle. Sharing an injection can pass infection or disease from person to person.

The Ozempic injection pen contains more than one dose. After your first use, store the pen with the needle removed in a refrigerator or at room temperature. Protect from heat and light. Keep the cap on when not in use. Throw the pen away 56 days after the first use or if less than 0.25 mg is shown on the dose counter.

Do not reuse a needle. Place it in a puncture-proof "sharps" container and dispose of it following state or local laws. Keep out of the reach of children and pets.

Semaglutide — Semaglutide dosing information
### Ozempic Dosing Information

#### Ozempic Injection Recommended Dosage

- Start Ozempic with a 0.25 mg subcutaneous injection (injection under the skin) once weekly for 4 weeks. The 0.25 mg dosage is intended for treatment initiation and is not effective for glycemic control.
- After 4 weeks on the 0.25 mg dosage, increase the dosage to 0.5 mg once weekly.
- If additional glycemic control is needed after at least 4 weeks on the 0.5 mg dosage, the dosage may be increased to 1 mg once weekly.
- If additional glycemic control is needed after at least 4 weeks on the 1 mg dosage, the dosage may be increased to 2 mg once weekly. The maximum recommended dosage is 2 mg once weekly.
- Administer Ozempic once weekly, on the same day each week, at any time of the day, with or without meals.
- The day of weekly administration can be changed if necessary, as long as the time between two doses is at least 2 days (>48 hours).
- If a dose is missed, administer Ozempic as soon as possible within 5 days after the missed dose. If more than 5 days have passed, skip the missed dose and administer the next dose on the regularly scheduled day. In each case, patients can then resume their regular once-weekly dosing schedule.

Ozempic is available as single-patient-use injections, and there are multiple strength options:

- 0.25 mg or 0.5 mg dose pen (4 doses of 0.25 mg and 2 doses of 0.5 mg, or 4 doses of 0.5 mg)
- 1 mg dose pen (4 doses per pen)
- 2 mg dose pen (4 doses per pen)

Each Ozempic pen carton contains 4 NovoFine Plus needles with 1mg and 2mg pens and 6 NovoFine Plus needles with the 0.25/0.5mg dose pen. Each injection is pre-filled and intended for use by one patient only.

#### Ozempic Pill Dosing Information

Starting Ozempic Dosage (Initiation phase):

- Days 1 through 30: 1.5 mg orally once daily (this dose is not effective for glycemic control).

Escalation and Maintenance Ozempic Dosage:

- Days 31 to 60: 4 mg orally once daily.
- Day 61 or thereafter if :

### Wegovy Dosing Information

#### Wegovy injection dosing information

Initiate Wegovy injection with a dose of 0.25 mg injected subcutaneously once weekly and follow the dose escalation schedule below to minimize gastrointestinal adverse reactions.

- Week 1 to week  4: 0.25mg once a week
- Week 5 to week 8: 0.5mg once a week
- Week 9 to week 12: 1mg once a week
- Week 13 to week 16: 1.7mg once a week

Adult weight reduction maintenance dose:

- 1.7 mg or 2.4 mg (recommended) injected subcutaneously once weekly.
- Dose may be increased to 7.2 mg subcutaneously once weekly on patients who tolerate the 2.4 mg dosage for at least 4 weeks and additional weight reduction is clinically indicated.
- Maximum dose 7.2 mg once weekly
- Consider treatment response and tolerability when selecting the maintenance dosage

Pediatric patients' weight reduction maintenance dose (patients aged 12 years and older):

- Either 2.4 mg (recommended) or 1.7 mg once weekly.
- Consider treatment response and tolerability when selecting the maintenance dosage

Cardiovascular risk reduction maintenance dose:

- Either 2.4 mg (recommended) or 1.7 mg once weekly, based on patient response and tolerance.

Non-cirrhotic metabolic dysfunction–associated steatohepatitis (MASH) with moderate to advanced liver fibrosis maintenance dose:

- 2.4 mg injected once weekly.
- If a patient does not tolerate the 2.4 mg once-weekly maintenance dose, the dose may be reduced to 1.7 mg once weekly.
- Re-escalation to 2.4 mg once weekly may be considered if the lower dose is tolerated.

In patients with type 2 diabetes, monitor blood glucose prior to starting Wegovy and during Wegovy treatment.

Wegovy Injections Strengths available:

- Wegovy 0.25 mg pen delivers one 0.25 mg dose (0.25 mg/0.5 mL)
- Wegovy 0.5 mg pen delivers one  0.5 mg dose (0.5 mg / 0.5 mL)
- Wegovy 1 mg pen delivers one 1 mg  dose (1 mg / 0.5 mL)
- Wegovy 1.7 mg pen delivers one 1.7 mg dose (1.7 mg / 0.75 mL)
- Wegovy 2.4 mg pen delivers one 2.4 mg dose (2.4 mg / 0.75 mL)
- Wegovy 7.2 mg pen delivers one 7.2 mg dose (7.2 mg/0.75 mL)

#### Wegovy Tablets Dosing Information

- Initial dose: 1.5 mg once daily for 30 days.
- Titration: Follow the dosage escalation schedule, titrating every 30 days to achieve the maintenance dosage.
- Maintenance dose: 25 mg orally once daily for cardiovascular risk reduction and weight reduction in adults.

### Rybelsus Dosing Information

Take Rybelsus at least 30 minutes before the first food, beverage, or other oral medications of the day with no more than 4 ounces of plain water only. After 30 minutes of taking the tablet, you may eat, drink, or take other oral medicines. Swallow tablets whole. Do not split, crush, or chew tablets.

Rybelsus Recommended Dosage R1 formulation

- Start Rybelsus with 3 mg once daily for 30 days. The 3 mg dose is intended for treatment initiation and is not effective for glycemic control.
- After 30 days on the 3 mg dose, increase the dose to 7 mg once daily.
- Dose may be increased to 14 mg once daily if additional glycemic control is needed after at least 30 days on the 7 mg dose.
- Taking two 7 mg Rybelsus tablets to achieve a 14 mg dose is not recommended.
- If a dose is missed, the missed dose should be skipped, and the next dose should be taken the following day.

Rybelsus tablets strengths are available in 3mg, 7mg, and 14mg

Rybelsus Recommended Dosage R2 formulation

- Start Rybelsus with 1.5 mg once daily for 30 days. The 1.5 mg dose is intended for treatment initiation and is not effective for glycemic control.
- After 30 days on the 1.5 mg dose, increase the dose to 4 mg once daily.
- Dose may be increased to 9 mg once daily if additional glycemic control is needed after at least 30 days on the 4 mg dose.
- If a dose is missed, the missed dose should be skipped, and the next dose should be taken the following day.

Rybelsus tablets strengths are available in 1.5 mg, 4 mg, and 9 mg

Switching Patients between Ozempic and Rybelsus

- Patients treated with Rybelsus 14 mg daily can be transitioned to Ozempic subcutaneous injection 0.5 mg once weekly. Patients can start Ozempic the day after their last dose of Rybelsus.
- Patients treated with once-weekly Ozempic 0.5 mg subcutaneous injection can be transitioned to Rybelsus 7 mg or 14 mg. Patients can start Rybelsus up to 7 days after their last injection of Ozempic. There is no equivalent dose of Rybelsus for Ozempic 1 mg.

Semaglutide — What happens if I miss a dose?
For Rybelsus: Skip the missed dose and use your next dose at the regular time.

For Ozempic injection: Use the medicine as soon as you can and then go back to your regular schedule. If you are more than 5 days late for the injection, skip the missed dose and return to your regular schedule.

For Wegovy injection: Use the medicine as soon as you can and then go back to your regular schedule. If your next dose is due in less than 2 days (48 hours), skip the missed dose and return to your regular schedule. Call your doctor if you miss more than 2 doses in a row of Wegovy injection. You may need to restart the medicine at a lower dose to avoid stomach problems.

For Wegovy tablets: If you miss a dose of Wegovy tablets, skip the missed dose and go back to your regular schedule for the next dose.

Do not use two doses of semaglutide at one time.

Semaglutide — What happens if I overdose?
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222.

Overdose may cause severe nausea , vomiting, or low blood sugar.

Semaglutide — What other drugs will affect semaglutide?
Semaglutide can slow your digestion, and it may take longer for your body to absorb any medicines you take by mouth.

Tell your doctor about all your other medicines, especially insulin or other diabetes medicines, such as dulaglutide , exenatide , liraglutide , Byetta , Trulicity , Victoza , and others.

Other drugs may affect semaglutide, including prescription and over-the-counter medicines, vitamins , and herbal products . Tell your doctor about all other medicines you use.

Semaglutide — Storage
Ozempic

- Prior to first use, Ozempic should be stored in a refrigerator between 36ºF to 46ºF (2ºC to 8ºC). Do not store in the freezer or directly adjacent to the refrigerator cooling element. Do not freeze Ozempic and do not use Ozempic if it has been frozen.
- After the first use of the Ozempic pen, the pen can be stored for 56 days at controlled room temperature (59°F to 86°F; 15°C to 30°C) or in a refrigerator (36°F to 46°F; 2°C to 8°C). Do not freeze.
- Keep the pen cap on when not in use. Ozempic should be protected from excessive heat and sunlight.
- Always remove and safely discard the needle after each injection and store the Ozempic pen without an injection needle attached. Always use a new needle for each injection.

Wegovy Injection

- Store the single-dose pen or syringe in the refrigerator from 2°C to 8°C (36°F to 46°F). Do not store Wegovy in the freezer or directly beside the refrigerator cooling element. Do not freeze Wegovy and do not use it if it has been frozen. If needed, prior to cap removal, the single-dose pen or syringe can be kept from 8°C to 30°C (46°F to 86°F) for up to 28 days. Protect Wegovy from light. Wegovy must be kept in the original carton until time of administration. Discard the Wegovy single-dose pen or syringe after use. For the Wegovy FlexTouch single-patient-use pen, store in the refrigerator until first use. After first use, store between 59°F to 86°F (15°C to 30°C) or in the refrigerator for up to 56 days. Keep the pen cap on when not in use and remove the needle after each injection.

Wegovy, Ozempic, Rybelsus Tablets

- Store Wegovy tablets at room temperature between 68°F and 77°F (20°C to 25°C) in their original closed bottle until you are ready to take one. Do not store in any other container.
- Store in a dry place away from moisture.

Semaglutide — What are the Ingredients of semaglutide?
Active ingredient : semaglutide Inactive ingredients :

Ozempic injection: disodium phosphate dihydrate, propylene glycol, phenol, and water for injections. Ozempic has a pH of approximately 7.4. Hydrochloric acid or sodium hydroxide may be added to adjust pH.

Ozempic tablet: magnesium stearate and salcaprozate sodium

Wegovy injection: disodium phosphate dihydrate, sodium chloride, and water for injection. Phenol and propylene glycol are also inactive ingredients in the single-patient-use Wegovy FlexTouch. Hydrochloric acid or sodium hydroxide may be added to adjust pH.

Wegovy tablets: salcaprozate sodium (SNAC) and magnesium stearate

Rybelsus R1 formulation: magnesium stearate, microcrystalline cellulose, povidone, and salcaprozate sodium (SNAC).

Rybelsus R2 formulation: magnesium stearate and salcaprozate sodium (SNAC).

Sertraline — What is sertraline?
Sertraline is a prescription medicine used to treat depression , obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD), panic disorders , posttraumatic stress disorder (PTSD), social anxiety disorder , and premenstrual dysphoric disorder (PMDD). It is sold under the brand name Zoloft , and generic sertraline is widely available.

Sertraline belongs to a group of medicines called selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors , or SSRIs. It increases serotonin activity in the brain by blocking the reuptake of serotonin (a chemical messenger that nerve cells use to communicate) back into nerve cells, so more of it stays available. Sertraline has only very weak effects on the reuptake of norepinephrine and dopamine, and it does not block monoamine oxidase.

Sertraline is available as tablets (25 mg, 50 mg, and 100 mg), oral solution (20 mg/mL), and capsules (150 mg and 200 mg).

Sertraline is not a controlled substance.

Sertraline — Boxed warning: suicidal thoughts and behaviors
Sertraline carries a boxed warning, the FDA's most serious type of warning. Antidepressants increased the risk of suicidal thoughts and behavior in children, teenagers, and young adults in short-term studies.

In pooled analyses of placebo-controlled antidepressant trials covering about 77,000 adults and more than 4,500 children and teenagers, the risk of suicidal thoughts and behaviors in people aged 24 and under was higher with an antidepressant than with placebo. The risk varied considerably between different antidepressants, and it also varied by condition, with the highest rate in people being treated for depression. The difference between antidepressant and placebo, per 1,000 patients treated, was:

Age (years) | Difference compared with placebo
Under 18 | 14 more patients with suicidal thoughts or behaviors
18 to 24 | 5 more patients
25 to 64 | 1 fewer patient
65 and over | 6 fewer patients

It is not known whether this risk continues beyond about four months of treatment. There is good evidence that antidepressants delay depression coming back in adults, and depression itself raises the risk of suicidal thoughts and behaviors.

Anyone taking sertraline should be watched closely for worsening symptoms and for new or sudden changes in mood, behavior, thoughts, or feelings, especially in the first few months of treatment and whenever the dose is changed. Call your healthcare provider right away, or call 911 in an emergency, if you notice:

- thoughts about suicide or dying, or attempts to harm yourself
- new or worse depression, anxiety, or panic attacks
- feeling agitated, restless, angry, or irritable
- acting aggressive or violent, or acting on dangerous impulses
- trouble sleeping
- a big increase in activity or talking more than is normal for you
- any other unusual change in behavior or mood

Tell family members or caregivers which symptoms to watch for, so they can call your provider if you are not able to.

Sertraline — Sertraline side effects
### Common sertraline side effects

The most common sertraline side effects are nausea, diarrhea or loose stools, trouble sleeping, dry mouth, tiredness, dizziness, sleepiness, tremor, indigestion, agitation, reduced appetite, increased sweating, and sexual problems.

The table below shows how often each side effect was reported in pooled placebo-controlled trials in 3,066 adults, compared with 2,293 people taking placebo.

Side effect | Sertraline | Placebo
Nausea | 26% | 12%
Diarrhea or loose stools | 20% | 10%
Insomnia (trouble sleeping) | 20% | 13%
Dry mouth | 14% | 9%
Fatigue (tiredness) | 12% | 8%
Dizziness | 12% | 8%
Somnolence (sleepiness) | 11% | 6%
Tremor (shaking) | 9% | 2%
Dyspepsia (indigestion) | 8% | 4%
Agitation | 8% | 5%
Decreased appetite | 7% | 2%
Hyperhidrosis (heavy sweating) | 7% | 3%
Constipation | 6% | 4%
Decreased libido (lower sex drive) | 6% | 2%
Palpitations | 4% | 2%
Visual impairment | 4% | 2%
Vomiting | 4% | 1%

Some side effects were more common with particular conditions. Sleepiness was more common in depression trials, insomnia and agitation in OCD trials, constipation and agitation in panic disorder trials, tiredness in PTSD trials, and insomnia, dizziness, tiredness, dry mouth, and a general feeling of being unwell in social anxiety disorder trials. In PMDD trials, sleepiness, dry mouth, dizziness, tiredness, and stomach pain were more common.

In these trials, 12% of people taking sertraline stopped treatment because of a side effect, compared with 4% of people taking placebo. The most common reasons for stopping were nausea (3%), diarrhea (2%), agitation (2%), and insomnia (2%).

### Sexual side effects of sertraline

SSRIs including sertraline can cause sexual problems. In men, this can mean delayed ejaculation or being unable to ejaculate, lower sex drive, and difficulty getting or keeping an erection. In women, it can mean lower sex drive and delayed orgasm or being unable to have an orgasm.

Sexual side effect | Sertraline | Placebo
Ejaculation failure (men) | 8% | 1%
Decreased libido (men) | 7% | 2%
Erectile dysfunction (men) | 4% | 1%
Ejaculation disorder (men) | 3% | 0%
Male sexual dysfunction | 2% | 0%
Decreased libido (women) | 4% | 2%

Changes in sex drive and sexual function can also be part of depression and anxiety themselves, so the timing of when symptoms started matters. These numbers may underestimate how often sexual side effects happen, because people and their providers do not always raise the subject. Tell your provider about any change in sexual function, since there may be ways to manage it.

### Serious sertraline side effects

Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction to sertraline: rash, hives, swelling of the face, lips, tongue, or throat, or difficulty breathing.

Call your doctor at once if you have:

- signs of serotonin syndrome , which can be life threatening: agitation, hallucinations, confusion, coma or other changes in mental state, a racing heartbeat, high or low blood pressure, dizziness, heavy sweating, fever, muscle stiffness, twitching or overactive reflexes, loss of coordination, seizures, nausea, vomiting, or diarrhea;
- unusual bleeding or bruising , including nosebleeds, bleeding gums, blood in vomit or stools, or black tarry stools;
- signs of a manic episode : greatly increased energy, severe trouble sleeping, racing thoughts, reckless behavior, unusually grand ideas, excessive happiness or irritability, or talking more or faster than usual;
- seizures or convulsions ;
- eye pain, changes in vision, or swelling or redness in or around the eye , which can be signs of angle-closure glaucoma (a sudden rise in pressure inside the eye);
- signs of low sodium in the blood (hyponatremia) : headache, trouble concentrating or thinking, memory problems, confusion, weakness, or feeling unsteady, which can lead to falls. Severe cases have caused hallucinations, fainting, seizures, coma, and death;
- fainting, chest discomfort, or an irregular heartbeat , which can be signs of a heart rhythm problem called QTc prolongation.

### Sertraline side effects in children and teenagers

The overall pattern of side effects in 281 children and teenagers treated with sertraline was generally similar to that seen in adults. Side effects reported in at least 2% of children and teenagers, and at least twice as often as with placebo, that were not common in adults were fever, hyperkinesia (abnormally increased movement), urinary incontinence, aggression, nosebleeds, purpura (purple spots under the skin), joint pain, weight loss, muscle twitching, and anxiety.

Reduced appetite and weight loss have been seen with SSRIs, so height and weight should be monitored during treatment.

### Other reported side effects

Less common side effects reported during studies include tachycardia (fast heartbeat), tinnitus (ringing in the ears), underactive thyroid, enlarged pupils, blurred vision, blood in the stools, swelling, unsteady walking, irritability, fever, raised liver enzymes, anaphylaxis, diabetes, high cholesterol, low blood sugar, increased appetite, joint pain, muscle spasms, poor coordination, coma, convulsions, reduced alertness, numbness, lethargy, restlessness, fainting, aggression, teeth grinding, confusion, euphoric mood, hallucination, blood in the urine, milk production from the breasts, priapism (a long-lasting erection), vaginal bleeding, wheezing, yawning, hair loss, cold sweats, skin inflammation, blistering, itching, rash, hives, bleeding, high blood pressure, and flushing.

Side effects reported after sertraline came onto the market include increased clotting times, AV block, slow heartbeat, atrial arrhythmias, QTc prolongation, ventricular tachycardia including Torsade de Pointes, breast enlargement in men, raised prolactin, irregular periods, SIADH, blindness, optic neuritis, cataract, severe liver problems including hepatitis, jaundice, and liver failure with some fatal outcomes, pancreatitis, several blood disorders, angioedema, low sodium, high blood sugar, rhabdomyolysis (muscle breakdown), lockjaw, serotonin syndrome, movement disorders including akathisia and dystonia, psychosis, bedwetting, nightmares, sudden kidney failure, pulmonary hypertension, eosinophilic pneumonia, loss of smell, photosensitivity, severe skin reactions such as Stevens-Johnson syndrome and toxic epidermal necrolysis, spasm of blood vessels in the brain, and vasculitis.

This is not a complete list of side effects, and others may occur. Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects. You may report side effects to the FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.

Sertraline — Before taking sertraline
You should not take sertraline if you are allergic to sertraline or any of the ingredients in it, if you take an MAOI or have taken one in the past 14 days, or if you take pimozide. Do not take the oral solution if you take disulfiram.

To make sure sertraline is safe for you, tell your doctor if you have or have ever had:

- liver problems
- kidney problems
- heart problems, or risk factors for a heart rhythm problem called QTc prolongation
- bipolar disorder or mania
- seizures or convulsions
- low sodium levels in your blood
- bleeding problems, or you take a medicine that thins the blood
- narrow angles in the eye or glaucoma
- a stroke or high blood pressure

### Pregnancy

Tell your doctor if you are pregnant, plan to become pregnant, or become pregnant while taking sertraline. Studies of women exposed to sertraline in the first trimester suggest no overall difference in the risk of major birth defects compared with the background rate. Some studies have reported increases in specific defects, but those results are inconclusive.

Taking sertraline late in pregnancy may raise the risk of newborn problems that need longer hospital stays, breathing support, and tube feeding, and of persistent pulmonary hypertension of the newborn (PPHN), a serious lung problem. Use in the month before delivery has been linked with a less than two-fold increase in the risk of postpartum hemorrhage (heavy bleeding after birth).

Stopping treatment is not automatically the safer choice. In one study of 201 pregnant women with a history of major depression, those who stopped their antidepressant during pregnancy were more likely to relapse than those who continued. This is a decision to make with your doctor.

Sertraline oral solution contains 12% alcohol and is not recommended during pregnancy, because there is no known safe level of alcohol exposure in pregnancy.

There is a pregnancy registry that collects information on antidepressant use in pregnancy. You can join by calling 1-866-961-2388.

### Breastfeeding

Low levels of sertraline and its breakdown products pass into breast milk. In a pooled analysis of 53 mother and infant pairs, exclusively breastfed infants had an average of 2% of the sertraline levels measured in their mothers' blood, ranging from 0% to 15%. No side effects were seen in those infants. There is no information on whether sertraline affects milk production. Talk with your doctor about the benefits of breastfeeding alongside your need for treatment.

### Children

Sertraline is approved for OCD in children aged 6 to 17 years. It is not known to be safe and effective in children under 6, or for any other condition in this age group. Children and teenagers taking sertraline should be monitored for worsening symptoms and suicidal thoughts, especially early in treatment and when the dose changes, and their weight and growth should be checked.

### Older adults

Older adults clear sertraline more slowly. In one study, plasma clearance in people aged 65 and over was about 40% lower than in adults aged 25 to 32, so it took 2 to 3 weeks to reach steady levels rather than about 1 week. Doctors usually start older adults at the low end of the dose range. Older adults are also at greater risk of low sodium levels in the blood, particularly if they take diuretics (water pills) or are dehydrated.

Sertraline — How should I take sertraline?
Take sertraline exactly as prescribed, once a day, in either the morning or the evening. Take it at around the same time each day.

- Tablets and capsules can be taken with or without food. Taking sertraline with food causes only a small increase in the amount absorbed.
- Swallow capsules whole. Do not open, crush, or chew them.
- Your doctor may start you on a low dose and raise it gradually, usually no more often than once a week.
- Keep taking sertraline even if you feel well, and do not stop without talking to your doctor first.
- Tell any doctor or lab that you take sertraline before you have tests. Sertraline can cause a false-positive result on urine screening tests for benzodiazepines, and this can continue for several days after you stop taking it. A confirmatory test will tell the difference.
- Store sertraline at room temperature, 68°F to 77°F (20°C to 25°C).

### How to take sertraline oral solution

Sertraline oral solution must be diluted before you take it. Do not mix it in advance.

- Use the dropper supplied with the bottle. The dropper has graduation marks for 25 mg and 50 mg only.
- Mix your dose with 4 ounces (half a cup) of water, ginger ale, lemon or lime soda, lemonade, or orange juice only. Do not mix it with any other liquid.
- Drink it straight away. A slight haze may appear after mixing, and this is normal.

The dropper dispenser contains dry natural rubber, a form of latex. Tell your doctor or pharmacist if you have a latex allergy.

Sertraline — What is the usual sertraline dosage?
The usual starting dose of sertraline is 25 mg or 50 mg once a day, depending on the condition being treated, and the usual treatment range is 50 mg to 200 mg a day. The maximum dose for most conditions is 200 mg a day.

Condition | Starting dose | Usual range or maximum
Depression (MDD), adults | 50 mg a day | 50 mg to 200 mg a day
OCD, adults | 50 mg a day | 50 mg to 200 mg a day
OCD, ages 6 to 12 | 25 mg a day | 50 mg to 200 mg a day
OCD, ages 13 to 17 | 50 mg a day | 50 mg to 200 mg a day
Panic disorder, PTSD, social anxiety disorder | 25 mg a day | 50 mg to 200 mg a day
PMDD, taken every day | 50 mg a day | Up to 150 mg a day
PMDD, taken in the luteal phase only | 50 mg a day | Up to 100 mg a day

If the starting dose is not enough, the dose can be raised in steps of 25 mg to 50 mg a day. Because sertraline stays in the body for about 24 hours, doses are usually changed no more often than once a week.

Dosing for PMDD. Sertraline can be taken every day of the menstrual cycle, or only during the luteal phase, meaning you start 14 days before your period is due and continue until it starts, then repeat with each cycle. On daily dosing, the dose can go up by 50 mg per cycle to a maximum of 150 mg a day. On luteal phase dosing, the maximum is 100 mg a day, taken as 50 mg for the first 3 days of the dosing cycle and 100 mg for the rest of it.

Sertraline 150 mg and 200 mg capsules. These are not used to start treatment or to titrate a dose, because there are no lower capsule strengths. They can be started in someone who has been taking 100 mg or 125 mg of another sertraline product for at least a week. The recommended capsule dose is 150 mg or 200 mg once a day, and the maximum is 200 mg a day. Because the dose cannot be lowered in steps using capsules alone, coming off treatment requires switching back to another sertraline product.

Liver problems. With sertraline tablets and oral solution, mild liver impairment (Child-Pugh score 5 or 6) means both the starting dose and the usual range are halved, and use is not recommended in moderate or severe liver impairment (Child-Pugh score 7 to 15). Sertraline capsules are not recommended in mild, moderate, or severe liver impairment, because the dose cannot be adjusted with the strengths available.

Kidney problems. No dose change is needed for mild, moderate, or severe kidney impairment, including in people on dialysis.

Sertraline — What happens if I miss a dose?
Take the missed dose as soon as you remember. If it is almost time for your next dose, skip the one you missed and take the next dose at the usual time. Do not take two doses at once.

Sertraline — What happens if I overdose?
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222.

Effects reported after a sertraline tablet overdose include seizures, which may be delayed, and changes in mental state including coma. Heart effects can also be delayed and include changes in the electrical activity of the heart (QRS and QTc prolongation). High blood pressure is seen most often, and low blood pressure is rare, though it can happen when other substances including alcohol have also been taken. Serotonin syndrome can occur, and the risk is higher when several drugs that raise serotonin have been taken together.

Sertraline — What should I avoid while taking sertraline?
- Do not drink alcohol while you take sertraline. In healthy volunteers, sertraline did not add to the immediate effects of alcohol on thinking and coordination, but drinking is still not recommended during treatment.
- Do not drive or operate machinery until you know how sertraline affects you. It can cause sleepiness and can affect your ability to think clearly, make decisions, or react quickly.
- Do not start or stop any other medicine without checking first , including over-the-counter products, vitamins, and herbal supplements.

Sertraline — What other drugs will affect sertraline?
Several medicines interact with sertraline. Give a full list of everything you take to any provider who treats you. The most important interactions are:

- MAOIs. Taking sertraline with an MAOI raises the risk of serotonin syndrome, and the combination is contraindicated. Examples include selegiline, tranylcypromine, isocarboxazid, phenelzine, linezolid, and methylene blue.
- Pimozide. Sertraline raises pimozide levels, which can lead to QTc prolongation and irregular heartbeats. The combination is contraindicated.
- Other medicines that raise serotonin. These add to the risk of serotonin syndrome. Examples include other SSRIs and SNRIs, triptans for migraine, tricyclic antidepressants, opioids, lithium, tryptophan, buspirone, amphetamines, and St. John's wort.
- Blood thinners and antiplatelet medicines. Aspirin, clopidogrel, heparin, and warfarin can add to the bleeding risk. If you take warfarin, your INR should be monitored carefully.
- Medicines broken down by CYP2D6. Sertraline blocks this liver enzyme, so levels of these drugs can rise and doses may need to be lowered. Examples include propafenone, flecainide, atomoxetine, desipramine, dextromethorphan, metoprolol, nebivolol, perphenazine, thioridazine, tolterodine, and venlafaxine.
- Phenytoin and fosphenytoin. Sertraline can raise phenytoin levels, so levels should be checked when sertraline is started or the dose is changed.
- Medicines that prolong the QTc interval. These should generally be avoided. Examples include certain antipsychotics (ziprasidone, iloperidone, chlorpromazine, mesoridazine, droperidol), certain antibiotics (erythromycin, gatifloxacin, moxifloxacin, sparfloxacin), Class 1A antiarrhythmics (quinidine, procainamide), Class III antiarrhythmics (amiodarone, sotalol), and others including pentamidine, methadone, halofantrine, mefloquine, dolasetron, probucol, and tacrolimus.
- Disulfiram , with the oral solution only, because of its alcohol content.

No dose adjustment is needed when sertraline is taken with cimetidine, diazepam, lithium, atenolol, tolbutamide, digoxin, or medicines broken down by CYP3A4.

This list is not complete. Many other prescription medicines, over-the-counter products, vitamins, and herbal products can interact with sertraline.

Simvastatin — What is simvastatin?
Simvastatin belongs to a group of drugs called HMG CoA reductase inhibitors, or "statins."

Simvastatin is used to lower blood levels of "bad" cholesterol (low-density lipoprotein, or LDL), to increase levels of "good" cholesterol (high-density lipoprotein, or HDL), and to lower triglycerides (a type of fat in the blood).

Simvastatin is also used to lower the risk of stroke, heart attack, and other heart complications in people with diabetes, coronary heart disease, or other risk factors.

Simvastatin is used in adults and children who are at least 10 years old.

Simvastatin — Warnings
In rare cases, simvastatin can cause a condition that results in the breakdown of skeletal muscle tissue, potentially leading to kidney failure. Call your doctor right away if you have unexplained muscle pain, tenderness, or weakness especially if you also have fever, unusual tiredness, and dark colored urine.

Never take simvastatin in larger amounts, or for longer than recommended by your doctor. Follow your doctor's dosing instructions very carefully. Taking too much of this medication may cause serious or life-threatening side effects.

Before taking this medicine, tell your doctor if you have ever had liver or kidney disease, diabetes, a thyroid disorder, a muscle weakness condition (such as myasthenia gravis or ocular myasthenia), if you are of Chinese descent, or if you drink more than 2 alcoholic beverages daily.

Simvastatin can harm an unborn baby or cause birth defects. Do not use if you are pregnant or planning to become pregnant.

Grapefruit and grapefruit juice may interact with simvastatin and lead to potentially dangerous effects. Do not consume grapefruit products while taking this medication.

Some medicines can cause unwanted or dangerous effects when used with simvastatin. Your doctor may need to change your treatment plan if you use certain antibiotics or antifungal medicines, heart medication, antiviral medications such as hepatitis C medications, HIV/AIDS medications, or Paxlovid for COVID-19.

Stop taking this medication and tell your doctor right away if you become pregnant.

Simvastatin — Before taking this medicine
You should not take this medicine if you are allergic to simvastatin, or if:

- you have active liver disease; or
- you are pregnant or breastfeeding.

The following drugs can increase your risk of serious muscle problems if you take them together with simvastatin. These drugs should not be used while you are taking this medicine:

- cyclosporine;
- danazol;
- gemfibrozil;
- nefazodone;
- an antibiotic - clarithromycin, erythromycin, telithromycin;
- antifungal medication - itraconazole, ketoconazole, posaconazole, voriconazole;
- hepatitis C medications - boceprevir, telaprevir; or
- HIV/AIDS medication - atazanavir, cobicistat (Stribild, Tybost), darunavir, fosamprenavir, indinavir, nelfinavir, ritonavir, saquinavir, tipranavir.

Before you start taking simvastatin, tell your doctor if you are already using any of these other medicines:

- lomitapide; or
- heart medication - amiodarone, amlodipine, diltiazem, dronedarone, ranolazine, verapamil.

To make sure simvastatin is safe for you, tell your doctor if you have:

- history of liver disease;
- history of kidney disease;
- diabetes;
- a thyroid disorder; or
- if you drink more than 2 alcoholic beverages daily
- a muscle weakness condition (such as myasthenia gravis or ocular myasthenia).

Simvastatin can cause a condition that results in the breakdown of skeletal muscle tissue, potentially leading to kidney failure. This condition may be more likely to occur in older adults and in people who have kidney disease or poorly controlled hypothyroidism (underactive thyroid).

This medicine can harm an unborn baby or cause birth defects. Do not take simvastatin if you are pregnant. Stop taking this medication and tell your doctor right away if you become pregnant. Use effective birth control to avoid pregnancy while you are taking this medicine.

Simvastatin may pass into breast milk and could harm a nursing baby. Do not breast-feed while you are taking this medicine.

Simvastatin — How should I take simvastatin?
Take simvastatin exactly as prescribed. Follow all directions on your prescription label. Never take this medicine in larger amounts, or for longer than prescribed. Taking too much of this medication may cause serious or life-threatening side effects.

Simvastatin is usually taken once a day in the evening. If you are taking the oral suspension, take it on an empty stomach. Your doctor may occasionally change your dose to make sure you get the best results.

If you are taking the oral suspension, shake the bottle well for at least 20 seconds before each use. Measure the medicine with an accurate measuring device, not a regular household spoon.

While using simvastatin, you may need frequent blood tests at your doctor's office.

You may need to take simvastatin on a long-term basis for the treatment of high cholesterol. You may need to stop using this medicine for a short time if you have surgery or a medical emergency. Do not stop taking this medicine unless your doctor tells you to.

Simvastatin is only part of a complete program of treatment that also includes diet, exercise, and weight control. Follow your diet, medication, and exercise routines very closely.

Store at room temperature away from moisture, heat, and light.

Simvastatin — What happens if I miss a dose?
Take the missed dose as soon as you remember. Skip the missed dose if it is almost time for your next scheduled dose. Do not take extra medicine to make up the missed dose.

Simvastatin — What happens if I overdose?
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222.

Simvastatin — What to avoid
Grapefruit and grapefruit juice may interact with simvastatin and lead to unwanted side effects. Avoid the use of grapefruit products while taking this medicine.

Avoid eating foods that are high in fat or cholesterol. Simvastatin will not be as effective in lowering your cholesterol if you do not follow a cholesterol-lowering diet plan.

Avoid drinking alcohol. It can raise triglyceride levels and may increase your risk of liver damage.

Simvastatin — Simvastatin side effects
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction to simvastatin (hives, difficult breathing, swelling in your face or throat) or a severe skin reaction (fever, sore throat, burning eyes, skin pain, red or purple skin rash with blistering and peeling).

Simvastatin can cause the breakdown of muscle tissue, which can lead to kidney failure. Call your doctor right away if you have unexplained muscle pain, tenderness, or weakness especially if you also have fever, unusual tiredness, or dark urine.

Also call your doctor at once if you have:

- signs of a kidney problem - little or no urinating; painful or difficult urination; swelling in your feet or ankles; feeling tired or short of breath; or
- muscle weakness in your hips, shoulders, neck, and back;
- trouble lifting your arms, trouble climbing or standing; or
- liver problems - nausea, upper stomach pain, itching, tired feeling, loss of appetite, dark urine, clay-colored stools, jaundice (yellowing of the skin or eyes).

Common simvastatin side effects may include:

- headache;
- constipation, nausea, stomach pain; or
- cold symptoms such as stuffy nose, sneezing, sore throat.

This is not a complete list of side effects and others may occur. Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects. You may report side effects to FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.

Simvastatin — What other drugs will affect simvastatin?
Many drugs can interact with simvastatin. Not all possible interactions are listed here. Tell your doctor about all your medications and any you start or stop using during treatment, especially:

- delavirdine;
- fenofibrate; or
- fluconazole.

Your doctor may need to change your treatment plan if you use certain antibiotics or antifungal medicines, heart medication, antiviral medications such as hepatitis C medications, HIV/AIDS medications, or Paxlovid for COVID-19. This list is not complete and many other drugs can interact with simvastatin. This includes prescription and over-the-counter medicines, vitamins, and herbal products. Give a list of all your medicines to any healthcare provider who treats you.

Spironolactone — What is spironolactone?
Spironolactone is a potassium-sparing diuretic (water pill) used for heart failure, high blood pressure, fluid build-up (edema) caused by liver or kidney disease, and a hormone condition called primary hyperaldosteronism. Spironolactone is sometimes prescribed off-label for women with acne or excessive facial hair growth (hirsutism).

Spironolactone works by blocking a hormone called aldosterone, which causes your body to pass more salt and water in your urine while keeping potassium in. This is why it is called potassium-sparing, and it is what makes spironolactone different from most other water pills, which flush potassium out.

Spironolactone also blocks androgens such as testosterone from binding to the androgen receptor, which explains several of its side effects, including breast tenderness and breast growth.

Spironolactone is FDA-approved to treat adults with:

- Heart failure (NYHA Class III-IV) with a reduced ejection fraction to help live longer, reduce swelling, and reduce the need for hospital stays. It is taken alongside other heart failure medicines rather than on its own.
- High blood pressure as an add-on treatment for people whose blood pressure is not controlled well enough by other medicines. Lowering blood pressure reduces the risk of stroke and heart attack.
- Fluid build-up (edema ) caused by cirrhosis of the liver when cutting back on fluid and salt has not been enough, and also for swelling caused by nephrotic syndrome (a kidney disorder) when treating the underlying disease and using other water pills have not worked well enough.
- Primary hyperaldosteronism, which is a condition where the adrenal glands make too much aldosterone. Spironolactone is used short-term to prepare for surgery, and long-term for people who are not suitable for surgery.

Spironolactone tablets come in 25 mg, 50 mg, and 100 mg strengths. Spironolactone 25 mg is the strength most often used to start treatment. There is also a banana-flavored oral liquid for people who cannot swallow tablets. The liquid and the tablets are not interchangeable, so never switch between them without your doctor changing your dose.

Spironolactone — Spironolactone side effects
### Reported spironolactone side effects

Reported spironolactone side effects include breast and nipple pain in both men and women, a lower sex drive, trouble getting or keeping an erection, irregular or missed periods, bleeding after menopause, dizziness, headache, drowsiness, feeling sluggish, leg cramps, nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, stomach cramps, indigestion, hair thinning, and itching.

The most common side effect of spironolactone is breast growth in men, known as gynecomastia. In the main heart failure study, about 9 in 100 men taking an average of 26 mg a day developed it. The chance goes up on higher doses. It can start anywhere from one or two months into treatment to more than a year later, and it usually goes away after the medicine is stopped.

Several of these effects are dose-related, so they are more likely on higher doses, and many settle after the dose is lowered or the medicine is stopped. How often most of them happen is not known, because they come from reports gathered after the medicine was approved rather than from counted numbers in trials.

### Serious spironolactone side effects

Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction to spironolactone: hives, difficulty breathing, or swelling of your face, lips, tongue, or throat.

Call your doctor at once if you have:

- signs of a high potassium level (hyperkalemia), such as muscle weakness or a heavy feeling in your legs, a slow or uneven heartbeat, numbness or tingling in your hands, feet, or lips, nausea, or feeling restless and on edge;
- a light-headed feeling like you might pass out, which can be a sign you have low blood pressure (hypotension)
- little or no urination, or swelling in your ankles, hands, or feet, which can be signs your kidneys are struggling (worsening renal function);
- signs of a low sodium level, such as confusion, severe weakness, muscle pain or cramps, feeling unsteady, or slurred speech;
- severe or ongoing vomiting or diarrhea, since losing too much fluid and salt can send your blood pressure and kidney function the wrong way;
- a skin rash that spreads or blisters, peeling skin, sores in your mouth, or a rash with a fever and swollen glands, which can be signs of a rare but serious skin reaction;
- yellowing of your eyes or skin, dark urine, or pain in your upper stomach, which can be signs of a liver problem;
- a fever, sore throat, or unusual bruising or bleeding, which can be signs of a problem with your blood cells; or
- new confusion, unsteadiness, or extreme drowsiness if you have liver disease, since spironolactone can make brain function worse in people with cirrhosis.

### Spironolactone side effects in men

Men are most likely to notice effects linked to spironolactone blocking male hormones. These include breast growth and breast tenderness, a lower sex drive, and difficulty getting or keeping an erection. Breast growth is dose related, so tell your doctor early rather than waiting, since a lower dose or a different medicine may be an option.

### Spironolactone side effects in women

Women may notice irregular periods, missed periods, breast tenderness, or bleeding after menopause. Some women also notice patchy darkening of the skin on the face. Report any unexpected bleeding after menopause to your doctor so it can be checked properly rather than assumed to be a side effect.

### Long-term spironolactone side effects

The main long-term concerns with spironolactone are chemical rather than something you would feel. Over time it can push your potassium too high, and it can also lower your sodium, magnesium, and calcium, raise your blood sugar, and raise your uric acid, which occasionally brings on gout . This is why you need regular blood tests for as long as you take it, not just at the start.

In animal studies, spironolactone given at high doses over long periods caused tumors in rats, mainly in hormone-producing glands and the liver. No increase in tumors was seen at the lower doses tested, which were still around five times the highest recommended human dose when adjusted for body size. Whether this happens in people is not known.

This is not a complete list of side effects, and others may occur. Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects. You may report side effects to the FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.

Spironolactone — Warnings
Do not take spironolactone if you have a high potassium level in your blood, if you have Addison's disease (an adrenal gland disorder), or if you are taking eplerenone (Inspra), another medicine of the same type.

Spironolactone can raise your potassium to a dangerous level. A high potassium level can cause a life-threatening change in your heart rhythm, and it often causes no symptoms until it is already high. Your doctor should check your potassium with a blood test within a week of you starting spironolactone or having your dose changed, and regularly after that.

Your risk of high potassium is greater if your kidneys do not work well, if you take potassium supplements or a salt substitute containing potassium, or if you take an ACE inhibitor, an ARB, an NSAID, heparin, or trimethoprim.

Do not use potassium supplements or salt substitutes containing potassium unless your doctor has specifically told you to.

Passing too much fluid can drop your blood pressure and make your kidney function worse, especially if you are also taking an ACE inhibitor or an ARB. Tell your doctor if you feel faint, or if you become unwell with vomiting or diarrhea.

If you have cirrhosis with fluid in your abdomen, spironolactone is usually started in hospital, because sudden shifts in fluid and salt can affect how your brain works.

Spironolactone should be avoided in pregnancy where possible, because it blocks male hormones and may affect how a male baby develops.

Spironolactone — Before taking this medicine
You should not use spironolactone if you are allergic to it, or if you have:

- a high level of potassium in your blood (hyperkalemia);
- Addison's disease (an adrenal gland disorder); or
- if you are taking eplerenone.

To make sure spironolactone is safe for you, tell your doctor if you have or have ever had:

- kidney disease, or reduced kidney function;
- liver disease, especially cirrhosis or fluid in the abdomen (ascites);
- an electrolyte imbalance, such as a low sodium, magnesium, or calcium level;
- dehydration, or any illness causing vomiting or diarrhea;
- diabetes, since spironolactone can raise blood sugar;
- gout or a high uric acid level; or
- prostate cancer treated with abiraterone.

### Pregnancy

Spironolactone blocks the effect of male hormones, and male hormones are needed for a male baby to develop normally. In animal studies, male offspring exposed in the womb showed feminized development. The limited information available in humans has not shown a clear increase in birth defects, but the concern remains. Your doctor will usually avoid spironolactone in pregnancy, or talk through the possible risk to a male baby with you. Untreated heart failure, cirrhosis, and high blood pressure also carry real risks in pregnancy, so this is a decision to make with your doctor rather than by stopping on your own.

### Breastfeeding

Spironolactone itself does not pass into breast milk. Its active breakdown product, canrenone, has been found in breast milk in very small amounts that are not expected to affect a breastfed baby. No side effects were reported in the one infant studied over a short period, and the long-term picture is not known. Talk to your doctor so you can weigh this up together.

### Children

Spironolactone has not been shown to be safe and effective in children. The oral liquid is specifically not recommended for treating high blood pressure in children, because of the hormonal effects of the medicine. Any use in a child must be decided and monitored by a specialist.

### Older adults

Spironolactone is cleared by the kidneys, and kidney function tends to fall with age. Older adults are therefore more likely to build up a high potassium level, so kidney function and potassium need monitoring.

Spironolactone — How should I take spironolactone?
Take spironolactone exactly as prescribed. Follow all directions on your prescription label and read all medication guides.

- Take it the same way each day, either always with food or always without. Food increases how much spironolactone your body absorbs, roughly doubling it, so switching back and forth changes how much medicine you actually get.
- Shake the oral liquid well before every dose, and measure it with the marked oral syringe, dosing spoon, or medicine cup that comes with it, not a kitchen spoon.
- Do not switch between tablets and the oral liquid without your doctor changing the dose. They are not interchangeable, and the liquid gives a higher level of medicine in your blood for the same number of milligrams.
- You will need regular blood tests to check your potassium, sodium, kidney function, uric acid, and blood sugar. Keep these appointments even if you feel completely well.
- If you are being treated for high blood pressure, keep taking spironolactone even when you feel fine. High blood pressure usually has no symptoms.
- Tell any doctor or dentist treating you that you take spironolactone, and tell your surgeon before any operation.
- Spironolactone can interfere with one type of blood test used to measure digoxin, so mention it if you take digoxin.
- Do not share this medicine with anyone else.
- Store tablets at room temperature, 68°F to 77°F (20°C to 25°C), and protect them from light. Store the oral liquid at room temperature and keep it in a tightly closed bottle.

Spironolactone — What is the usual spironolactone dosage?
The usual starting dose of spironolactone tablets for adults is 25 mg to 100 mg a day, depending on the condition being treated. Your doctor will pick a starting dose and then adjust it based on your blood tests and how you respond.

### Spironolactone dosage for heart failure

Treatment starts at spironolactone 25 mg once a day, provided your potassium level is 5.0 mEq/L or lower and your kidney function (eGFR) is above 50. If you tolerate that, the dose may go up to 50 mg once a day. If your potassium rises too high, the dose may drop to 25 mg every other day. If your eGFR is between 30 and 50, your doctor may start you at 25 mg every other day because of the higher risk of high potassium.

### Spironolactone dosage for high blood pressure

The usual starting dose is spironolactone 25 mg to 100 mg a day, taken as one dose or split into two. The dose can be adjusted every two weeks. Doses above 100 mg a day do not generally lower blood pressure any further.

### Spironolactone dosage for fluid build-up (edema)

The usual starting dose is 100 mg a day, taken as one dose or split into two, and the dose can range from 25 mg to 200 mg a day. If you have cirrhosis, treatment is started in hospital and increased slowly. When spironolactone is the only water pill being used, it is given for at least five days before the dose is increased.

### Spironolactone dosage for primary hyperaldosteronism

The dose is 100 mg to 400 mg a day when preparing for surger y. For people who are not suitable for surgery, the lowest dose that works is used long term.

### Spironolactone oral liquid doses

The oral liquid has its own separate doses because it is absorbed differently. Heart failure starts at 20 mg (4 mL) once a day. High blood pressure starts at 20 mg (4 mL) to 75 mg (15 mL) a day. Swelling from cirrhosis starts at 75 mg (15 mL) a day, begun in hospital. If more than 100 mg a day is needed, a different form of the medicine should be used instead.

### Spironolactone formulations

- Spironolactone 25 mg tablets
- Spironolactone 50 mg tablets
- Spironolactone 100 mg tablets
- Spironolactone oral suspension 25 mg per 5 mL

Spironolactone — What happens if I miss a dose?
Take the missed dose as soon as you remember. Skip it if it is almost time for your next dose. Do not take two doses at once to make up for a missed one.

Spironolactone — What happens if I overdose?
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222.
