Taking too much spironolactone can cause drowsiness, confusion, a rash, nausea, vomiting, dizziness, or diarrhea. A dangerously high potassium level is the main serious risk, particularly if your kidneys do not work well.

Spironolactone — What should I avoid while taking spironolactone?
- Avoid potassium supplements, salt substitutes containing potassium, and low-sodium products that use potassium instead of salt. Check labels, since potassium chloride is a common salt replacement.
- Go easy on foods very high in potassium if your doctor has told you to. This is worth asking about specifically, because most people are told to eat more potassium, not less.
- Be careful with NSAIDs such as ibuprofen and naproxen. They can push your potassium up, make spironolactone work less well, and strain your kidneys.
- Limit alcohol. It can add to the dizziness and light-headedness, especially when you first start.
- Avoid getting up quickly from sitting or lying down, and avoid driving or anything hazardous until you know how spironolactone affects you.
- Do not take another medicine containing spironolactone at the same time, such as Aldactazide, and do not take eplerenone.

Spironolactone — What other drugs will affect spironolactone?
The most important interactions with spironolactone are medicines that also raise potassium, because the combined effect can push your level dangerously high. Tell your doctor about every medicine you take, including anything you start or stop during treatment. Be especially careful with:

- eplerenone , which should not be taken with spironolactone at all;
- medicines that raise potassium , including ACE inhibitors such as lisinopril and ramipril, ARBs such as losartan and valsartan, NSAIDs, heparin and low molecular weight heparin, and trimethoprim;
- potassium supplements and potassium-containing salt substitutes , which are usually stopped when spironolactone is started for heart failure;
- lithium , since spironolactone slows how fast your kidneys clear it and can cause lithium toxicity, so lithium levels need monitoring;
- digoxin , which needs care for two separate reasons: spironolactone interferes with one type of blood test used to measure digoxin, and it can also raise the actual amount of digoxin in your body;
- NSAIDs , which can also make spironolactone less effective as a water pill and as a blood pressure medicine;
- aspirin , which can reduce how well spironolactone works, so your dose may need adjusting;
- cholestyramine , which together with spironolactone has caused a high potassium level with too much acid in the blood;
- abiraterone , used in prostate cancer, which should not be combined with spironolactone because spironolactone attaches to the androgen receptor and can raise PSA readings;
- mitotane , which should be avoided because spironolactone lowers its level in the blood; and
- other blood pressure medicines and water pills , which add to the blood pressure lowering effect.

This list is not complete. Other prescription and over-the-counter medicines, vitamins, and herbal products can interact with spironolactone. Give a list of all your medicines to any healthcare provider who treats you.

Tamsulosin — What is tamsulosin?
Tamsulosin is used to treat the symptoms of an enlarged prostate, also called benign prostatic hyperplasia or BPH . These symptoms include a weak urine stream, trouble starting to urinate, dribbling, a feeling of not emptying the bladder completely, needing to urinate often or urgently, and getting up at night to urinate.

Tamsulosin belongs to a group of medicines called alpha blockers. It works by blocking alpha-1 receptors in the prostate and bladder neck. Blocking these receptors relaxes the muscle there, which improves urine flow and eases BPH symptoms.

Tamsulosin is taken once daily, about half an hour after the same meal each day. Capsules should be swallowed whole and not crushed, chewed, or opened.

The tamsulosin brand name was Flomax, which has now been discontinued in the U.S., but generic tamsulosin is available.

Tamsulosin is not approved to treat high blood pressure, and it is not approved for use in women or children.

Tamsulosin — Warnings and precautions
Do not take tamsulosin if you are allergic to tamsulosin or to any of the ingredients in the capsule.

Do not take tamsulosin with other alpha blockers such as alfuzosin (Uroxatral), doxazosin (Cardura), prazosin (Minipress), silodosin (Rapaflo), or terazosin (Hytrin).

Do not take tamsulosin 0.4 mg with a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor such as ketoconazole. These medicines raise tamsulosin levels in your blood.

Tamsulosin can lower your blood pressure when you stand up, which may make you feel dizzy or faint. This is most likely when you first start taking it, when your dose changes, or when you start it again after a break. Get up slowly from sitting or lying down. Be careful driving or doing anything that needs you to be alert until you know how tamsulosin affects you.

In rare cases (probably fewer than 1 in 50,000 patients), tamsulosin can cause priapism, a painful erection that lasts a long time and is not related to sexual activity. Get medical help straight away if this happens, because without prompt treatment it can lead to permanent erectile dysfunction.

An enlarged prostate and prostate cancer can occur together. Ask your doctor about prostate cancer screening before you start tamsulosin and at regular intervals while you are taking it.

Tamsulosin can cause a problem called intraoperative floppy iris syndrome (IFIS) during cataract or glaucoma surgery. Tell your eye surgeon that you take tamsulosin or have taken it in the past, even if you stopped months ago. Do not stop taking tamsulosin before surgery unless your surgeon tells you to. If you are already booked in for cataract or glaucoma surgery, starting tamsulosin is not recommended.

If you stop taking tamsulosin for several days, talk to your doctor before starting again. You will need to restart at the 0.4 mg dose.

Many other medicines can interact with tamsulosin. Tell your doctor about everything you take, including prescription medicines, over-the-counter medicines, vitamins, and herbal products.

Tamsulosin — Before taking this medicine
To make sure tamsulosin is safe for you, tell your doctor if you have ever had:

- an allergy to tamsulosin or any of its ingredients
- an allergy to sulfa drugs
- kidney problems
- liver problems
- low blood pressure (hypotension)

Also tell your doctor if you are planning to have cataract or glaucoma surgery.

Ask your doctor about prostate cancer screening before starting tamsulosin and at regular intervals afterwards.

Tamsulosin is not approved for use in women or in children.

Tamsulosin — How should I take tamsulosin?
Take tamsulosin exactly as prescribed. Follow the directions on your prescription label and read any medication guides or instruction sheets.

- Take once a day, about 30 minutes after the same meal each day. For example, 30 minutes after dinner.
- Swallow the capsule whole. Do not crush, chew, break, or open it.
- If you stop taking tamsulosin for several days, do not start again without talking to your doctor.
- Store at room temperature, away from moisture and heat.

### Dosing information

Usual adult dose for benign prostatic hyperplasia:

- 0.4 mg by mouth once a day, taken about half an hour after the same meal each day.
- If symptoms have not improved after 2 to 4 weeks at 0.4 mg, the dose may be increased to 0.8 mg once a day.
- Tamsulosin 0.4 mg should not be taken with a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor such as ketoconazole.
- If tamsulosin is stopped or interrupted for several days, treatment should start again at 0.4 mg once a day.

Tamsulosin — What happens if I miss a dose?
Take the missed dose as soon as you remember. If you have missed the dose for the whole day, skip it and take your next dose at the usual time. Do not take two doses at once.

If you stop taking tamsulosin for several days in a row, talk to your doctor before starting again.

Tamsulosin — What happens if I overdose?
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222. An overdose can cause a serious drop in blood pressure. Lying down flat can help until medical help arrives.

Tamsulosin — What should I avoid while taking tamsulosin?
Avoid driving or other activities that could be dangerous until you know how tamsulosin affects you. It can make you feel dizzy or lightheaded.

Avoid getting up too quickly from sitting or lying down, or you may feel dizzy.

Tamsulosin — Tamsulosin side effects
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction, such as hives, itching, rash, swelling of your face, tongue, or throat, or trouble breathing. Rarely, tamsulosin has been linked to severe skin reactions with blistering and peeling of the skin.

Stop taking tamsulosin and call your doctor at once if you have:

- a lightheaded feeling, like you might pass out
- a painful erection that will not go away and is not related to sexual activity

### What are the common side effects of tamsulosin?

Common tamsulosin side effects may include:

- headache
- dizziness
- runny or stuffy nose, sore throat, cough, sinus congestion
- flu-like symptoms or infection
- weakness or lack of energy
- back pain, chest pain
- diarrhea, nausea
- drowsiness or trouble sleeping
- tooth problems
- abnormal ejaculation, including a smaller amount of semen or no semen at all
- reduced interest in sex
- blurred vision

Some side effects are more likely at the higher 0.8 mg dose than at the usual 0.4 mg starting dose. In clinical studies, abnormal ejaculation was reported by about 8 in 100 men taking 0.4 mg and about 18 in 100 men taking 0.8 mg. Blurred vision was reported by about 2 in 100 men taking 0.8 mg, and was uncommon at 0.4 mg.

This is not a complete list of side effects and others may occur. Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects. You may report side effects to FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.

Tamsulosin — What other drugs will affect tamsulosin?
Tamsulosin is broken down in the body mainly by two liver enzymes, CYP3A4 and CYP2D6. Medicines that block these enzymes can raise the amount of tamsulosin in your blood and increase the risk of side effects.

Do not take tamsulosin with:

- strong CYP3A4 inhibitors, such as ketoconazole
- other alpha blockers, such as alfuzosin, doxazosin, prazosin, silodosin, or terazosin

Use with caution, and tell your doctor if you take:

- moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors, such as erythromycin
- strong CYP2D6 inhibitors, such as paroxetine, or moderate CYP2D6 inhibitors, such as terbinafine
- cimetidine
- warfarin
- medicines for erectile dysfunction or pulmonary arterial hypertension known as PDE5 inhibitors, including avanafil (Stendra), sildenafil (Viagra, Revatio), tadalafil (Adcirca, Cialis), and vardenafil (Levitra). Taking these with tamsulosin can lower blood pressure enough to cause symptoms.

Some of these cautions matter more at the higher 0.8 mg dose than at 0.4 mg. Your doctor will decide what is right for you.

No dose change is needed if you take tamsulosin with nifedipine, atenolol, enalapril, digoxin, theophylline, or furosemide.

This list is not complete, and many other medicines may interact with tamsulosin. This includes prescription and over-the-counter medicines, vitamins, and herbal products.

Tirzepatide — What is Tirzepatide?
Tirzepatide is a once-weekly injection available as two brands: Zepbound (for weight loss, weight management, and obstructive sleep apnea) and Mounjaro (for type 2 diabetes and to lower the risk of major adverse cardiovascular events in those with type 2 diabetes at high risk).

Zepbound (tirzepatide) is FDA-approved for weight loss and weight management in adults with obesity, or in overweight adults with at least one weight-related medical condition. Zepbound is also approved to treat moderate-to-severe obstructive sleep apnea (OSA) in adults with obesity.

Mounjaro (tirzepatide) is FDA-approved for type 2 diabetes in adults and children aged 10 years and older to help improve blood sugar (glucose) levels, used alongside diet and exercise. It is also approved to lower the risk of major adverse cardiovascular events (cardiovascular death, non-fatal heart attack, or non-fatal stroke) in adults with type 2 diabetes who are at high risk for these events.

Tirzepatide is a dual GIP/ GLP-1 receptor agonist , meaning it activates two gut hormone receptors that help regulate appetite, digestion, and blood sugar. It works by reducing appetite, increasing feelings of fullness, slowing gastric emptying, boosting insulin release when blood sugar is high, and reducing glucagon secretion.

Tirzepatide is self-administered as a subcutaneous (under the skin) injection using a single-dose pen, KwikPen, or vial with syringe, and should always be used together with diet and exercise.

Tirzepatide should not be used in people with type 1 diabetes. It is not known if tirzepatide is safe in people who have had pancreatitis.

Tirzepatide — Tirzepatide side effects
### Common Side Effects

The most common tirzepatide side effects are:

- Diarrhea
- Vomiting
- Constipation
- Nausea (mild to moderate)
- Decreased appetite
- Abdominal pain.

### Serious side effects

Tirzepatide can cause serious side effects. Call your doctor right away if you have any of the following:

- Severe stomach pain (pancreatitis): Stop taking tirzepatide and call your healthcare provider immediately if you have severe pain in your stomach area that will not go away, with or without vomiting, or pain that spreads to your back.
- Kidney problems: Diarrhea, nausea, and vomiting can cause dehydration, which may lead to kidney problems. Drink plenty of fluids to reduce this risk and tell your doctor if you are unable to keep fluids down.
- Gallbladder problems: Symptoms include severe upper stomach pain, fever, nausea, vomiting, yellowing of the skin or eyes (jaundice), or clay-colored stools.
- Serious allergic reactions: Seek emergency medical attention if you experience hives, difficulty breathing, feeling light-headed, or swelling of your face, lips, tongue, or throat (anaphylaxis or angioedema).
- Low blood sugar (hypoglycemia): Symptoms may include headache, hunger, weakness, sweating, confusion, irritability, dizziness, fast heart rate, or feeling jittery.
- Vision changes: Tell your doctor about any blurred vision or other vision changes, particularly if you have type 2 diabetes.
- Thyroid tumor: Symptoms may include a lump or swelling in your neck, trouble swallowing, hoarseness, or shortness of breath.
- Mental health changes: Tell your healthcare provider right away about any new or worsening depression, mood changes, or thoughts of suicide or self-harm.
- Aspiration during surgery: Tirzepatide slows stomach emptying, which may increase the risk of food or liquid entering the lungs during procedures using anesthesia or sedation. Tell all your healthcare providers you are taking tirzepatide before any planned surgery or procedure.

This is not a complete list of side effects. Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects, or report them to the FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.

Tirzepatide — Important Safety Information
Who should not use tirzepatide:

- Type 1 diabetes patients (tirzepatide is not approved for type 1 diabetes)
- Personal or family history of medullary thyroid carcinoma (MTC)
- Multiple Endocrine Neoplasia syndrome type 2 (MEN 2).

Use with Caution:

- History of pancreatitis (safety not established in patients with prior pancreatitis)
- Severe gastrointestinal disease
- Diabetic retinopathy complications.

Tirzepatide — Before using tirzepatide
- Tell your doctor and pharmacist if you are allergic to tirzepatide, any other medications, or any of the ingredients in this medicine.  There is a list of ingredients in this medicine, at the bottom of this page.
- Tell your healthcare team if you are planning surgery or other procedure that uses anesthesia or deep sedation.
- Tell your doctor if you have or have ever had kidney or pancreas disease, a history of diabetic retinopathy or any stomach problems such as difficulty digesting food.
- Tell your doctor if you plan to become pregnant, are pregnant, or are breastfeeding. If you become pregnant while taking this medicine, you should tell your doctor. There is a pregnancy exposure registry for women who take Zepbound during pregnancy. Talk to your healthcare provider about how you can take part, call 1-844-524-0039, or visit https://pregnancyregistry.lilly.com/zepbound.

Tirzepatide — How should I use tirzepatide?
You should follow the directions on your prescription label carefully and ask your pharmacist or doctor to explain any part you do not understand. It is important to use this medicine exactly as directed. Do not take more or less of it or take it more often than prescribed by your doctor.

Tirzepatide is injected under the skin (subcutaneously) using a pre-filled single-dose pen, a multi-dose KwikPen (for single patient use), and a single-dose vial with a syringe. Caregivers or patients should be trained on proper injection techniques and should read the instructions for use . Children should not inject themselves with the KwikPen; an adult caregiver should give a child their injections.

Tirzepatide is usually given:

- once a week.
- can be given with or without meals at any time of the day.
- may be injected into the thigh, abdomen, or have another person inject it into the back of your upper arm
- You should rotate injection sites with each dose.
- Do not inject into a muscle (intramuscularly) or vein (intravenously).
- Do not mix insulin and tirzepatide together in the same injection. You may give an injection of tirzepatide and insulin in the same body area, but not right next to each other.

Your doctor will probably start you on a low dose, which may be gradually increased, but not more than once every 4 weeks.

You may change the day of the week you use tirzepatide as long as there are at least three days between doses.

Do not stop taking this medicine without talking to your doctor.

### Tirzepatide KwikPen

The KwikPen contains 4 fixed doses, and one dose is injected weekly (subcutaneously).

- For your weekly dose, you need to turn the dose knob all the way until it stops AND the 1 icon is in the dose window.
- After 4 doses, throw away (discard) the Pen, including the unused medicine. The Pen will prevent you from dialing a full dose after you have given yourself 4 weekly doses. Do not inject the leftover medicine. Do not transfer the leftover medicine from your Pen into a syringe.
- Do not share your tirzepatide Pen with other people, even if the pen needle has been changed. You may give other people a serious infection or get a serious infection from them.
- People who are blind or have vision problems should not use the Pen without help from a person who has been trained to use the pen.

Tirzepatide — Dosing Information
### Usual Zepbound Dose for Weight Loss and OSA (Adult)

- Initial dose: 2.5 mg under the skin (subcutaneously) once a week.
- After 4 weeks: The dosage should be increased to 5 mg subcutaneously once a week.
- Further dose increases: After at least 4 weeks on the current dose, the dosage may be increased in 2.5 mg increments.
- Recommended maintenance dose for weight loss: 5 mg, 10 mg, or 15 mg injected subcutaneously once weekly.
- Recommended maintenance dose for OSA : 10 mg or 15 mg injected subcutaneously once weekly.
- Maximum dose: 15 mg subcutaneously once a week.

### Dose of Mounjaro for Type 2 Diabetes (Adults and Children 10 Years and Older)

- Initial dose: 2.5 mg subcutaneously once a week.
- If additional glycemic control is needed: Increase the dose in 2.5 mg increments after at least 4 weeks on the current dose.
- Maximum dose (adults): 15 mg subcutaneously once a week.
- Maximum dose (children 10 years and older): 10 mg subcutaneously once a week.
- Comments: The starting dose and escalation schedule are designed to reduce the risk of gastrointestinal side effects.

### Dose of Mounjaro to Reduce Cardiovascular Risk (Adults With Type 2 Diabetes at High Risk)

- The same dosing and escalation schedule applies.
- Start at 2.5 mg subcutaneously once a week and increase in 2.5 mg increments after at least 4 weeks on the current dose, up to a maximum of 15 mg once a week.
- In the SURPASS-CVOT trial, participants were titrated to 15 mg once weekly or their maximum tolerated dose.

### How to Use Mounjaro

- Give once weekly at any time of day, with or without meals.
- Inject subcutaneously into the abdomen or thigh. Another person may inject into the back of the upper arm. Rotate injection sites with each dose.
- Missed dose: Give as soon as possible within 4 days (96 hours). If more than 4 days have passed, skip the missed dose and give the next dose on the regularly scheduled day.
- The day of weekly administration can be changed, if necessary, as long as the time between the 2 doses is at least 3 days (72 hours).

Tirzepatide — What should I do if I miss a dose?
If you miss a dose of tirzepatide, take the missed dose as soon as you remember it, if within 4 days after the missed dose. However, if it has been more than 4 days since your last dose, then skip the missed dose and continue your regular dosing schedule. Do not inject two doses within 3 days of each other.

Tirzepatide — What happens if I overdose?
In case of overdose, call the poison control helpline at 1-800-222-1222. Information is also available online at Poison Help. If the victim has collapsed, had a seizure, has trouble breathing, or can't be awakened, immediately call emergency services at 911.

Tirzepatide — Interactions
Tell your doctor and pharmacist what prescription and over-the-counter medications, vitamins, nutritional supplements, and herbal products you are planning to take or are taking. They may interact with tirzepatide, and your doctor may need to change the doses of your medications or monitor you carefully for side effects.

Birth control pills may not work as well while receiving this medicine. Your doctor may suggest you use another form of birth control for 4 weeks after starting tirzepatide and for 4 weeks after each dose change.

Taking tirzepatide with other medicines to treat diabetes, such as sulfonylureas or insulin, may increase your risk of hypoglycemia. Talk to your doctor and pharmacist about low blood sugar and how to manage it.

This medicine delays gastric emptying and may alter the absorption of other orally administered medicines.

This list is not complete. Many other drugs may interact with Mounjaro and Zepbound, including prescription and over-the-counter medicines, vitamins, and herbal products. To check for interactions with this medicine, click the link below.

Tirzepatide — Storage
Single-dose pens and vials (Zepbound and Mounjaro)

- Store in the refrigerator between 36°F and 46°F (2°C and 8°C).
- Do not freeze. Do not use if the medication has been frozen.
- Keep in the original carton until use to protect from light.
- If needed, single-dose pens and vials may be kept at room temperature up to 86°F (30°C) for up to 21 days. Once at room temperature, do not return to the refrigerator.
- Discard after 21 days at room temperature if unused.

KwikPen (Zepbound KwikPen and Mounjaro KwikPen)

Before first use:

- Store in the refrigerator between 36°F and 46°F (2°C and 8°C).
- Keep until the expiration date printed on the label.
- Do not freeze. Discard the pen if it has been frozen.

After first use:

- Store in the refrigerator or at room temperature up to 86°F (30°C).
- Keep away from heat and light.
- Discard the pen 30 days after first use, even if medicine remains.
- Discard after 4 weekly doses have been given.

General storage advice (all tirzepatide products)

- Keep all pens, vials, needles, and syringes out of reach of children.
- Dispose of used pens, needles, and syringes in an approved sharps container.

Tirzepatide — Tirzepatide ingredients
Zepbound and Mounjaro contain the same active ingredient, tirzepatide, and share the same inactive ingredients.

Active ingredient: tirzepatide

Inactive ingredients (single-dose pens and vials):

- Sodium chloride
- Sodium phosphate dibasic heptahydrate
- Water for injection
- Hydrochloric acid and/or sodium hydroxide (added to adjust pH)

Additional inactive ingredients (KwikPen and multi-dose vial only):

- Benzyl alcohol
- Glycerin
- Phenol

Tramadol — What is tramadol?
Tramadol is an opioid agonist that may be used to treat moderate to moderately severe chronic pain in adults, when other pain treatments such as non-opioid pain medicines do not treat your pain well enough, or you cannot tolerate them.

- Extended-release forms of tramadol may be used in adults who require around-the-clock treatment of their pain for an extended period.
- An opioid pain medicine such as tramadol can put you at risk for overdose and death. Even if you take your dose correctly as prescribed, you are at risk for opioid addiction, abuse, and misuse that can lead to death.

Brands of tramadol include ConZip, Qdolo, Ryzolt, Ultram, Ultram ER, and Ultracet. Many have been discontinued, but generic tramadol is still available .

Taking tramadol in combination with other pain-relieving medicines such as acetaminophen, ibuprofen, or aspirin can provide better pain relief than just taking one pain-relieving medicine by itself (see Can you take tramadol in combination with acetaminophen, ibuprofen, or aspirin? )

Tramadol first gained FDA approval on March 3, 1995. Originally, it was classified as a non-controlled substance, but it was reclassified in 2014 to Schedule IV of the Controlled Substances Act with restrictions on its prescribing . Tramadol is an opioid (see Is tramadol an opioid? ).

A person's genetics can affect the pain-relieving properties of tramadol (see How do genetics affect tramadol? ).

Tramadol — Important Information About Tramadol
Get emergency help or call 911 right away if you take too many tramadol (overdose). When you first start taking tramadol, when your dose is changed, or if you take too much (overdose), serious or life-threatening breathing problems that can lead to death may occur. Ask your healthcare provider about medicines like naloxone or nalmefene that can be used in an emergency to reverse an opioid overdose.

Taking tramadol with other opioid medicines , benzodiazepines, gabapentinoids (gabapentin or pregabalin), alcohol, or other central nervous system depressants (including street drugs) can cause severe drowsiness, decreased awareness, breathing problems, coma, and death.

Do not take tramadol with any other medicine that contains tramadol . Check the label of every pain medicine you take, as some combination products contain tramadol.

You should be given a Medication Guide each time you fill a tramadol prescription . Read it every time, even if you have taken tramadol before, because the information may have changed. Your healthcare provider or pharmacist should also go through the risks of tramadol and how to take it safely with you, and explain what to do in an emergency.

Never give anyone else your tramadol . They could die from taking it. Selling or giving away tramadol is against the law.

Store tramadol securely , out of sight and reach of children, and in a location not accessible by others, including visitors to the home.

Tramadol — Tramadol side effects
### Common side effects

The most common side effects of tramadol are:

- dizziness or vertigo
- nausea
- constipation
- headache
- drowsiness
- vomiting
- itching.

Dry mouth is also commonly reported with the extended-release forms of tramadol.

A person's genetics can affect their response to tramadol, including their risk of side effects (see How do genetics affect tramadol? ).

### Serious tramadol side effects and warnings

Tramadol may cause serious side effects , some of which may be fatal.

Tramadol can slow or stop your breathing and may be habit-forming . MISUSE OF THIS MEDICINE CAN CAUSE ADDICTION, OVERDOSE, OR DEATH, especially in a child or other person using the medicine without a prescription. Keep this medicine where others cannot get to it. Do not take more tramadol than prescribed, as an overdose can be fatal. Selling or giving away this medicine is against the law.

- A person caring for you should give naloxone and seek emergency medical attention if you have slow breathing with long pauses, blue-colored lips, or if you find it hard to wake up. Serious breathing problems may be more likely in older adults and people who are debilitated or have wasting syndrome or chronic breathing disorders.
- Call your doctor at once if you have: noisy breathing, sighing, shallow breathing, breathing that stops during sleep a slow heart rate or weak pulse a light-headed feeling, like you might pass out seizure (convulsions) or low cortisol levels (symptoms include nausea, vomiting, loss of appetite, dizziness, worsening tiredness or weakness) low sodium levels (hyponatremia). Symptoms include headache, confusion, weakness, unsteadiness, or feeling sick low blood sugar (hypoglycemia). Symptoms include shakiness, sweating, hunger, confusion, or a fast heartbeat.

Seek medical attention right away if you have symptoms of serotonin syndrome , such as agitation, hallucinations, fever, sweating, shivering, fast heart rate, muscle stiffness, twitching, loss of coordination, nausea, vomiting, or diarrhea.

Seizures have been reported in patients taking tramadol. Your risk of seizures is higher if you are taking higher doses than recommended. Seizure risk is also higher in those with epilepsy or a history of seizures, and in those taking certain antidepressants, other opioids, MAO inhibitors, or other medicines that lower the seizure threshold.

You should not take tramadol if you have significant breathing problems , acute or severe asthma in a setting where you cannot be monitored, a blockage in your stomach or intestines, or if you have taken an MAO inhibitor in the past 14 days (isocarboxazid, linezolid, methylene blue injection, phenelzine, tranylcypromine, and others). Tell your doctor before starting tramadol if you have a head injury or increased pressure inside your skull, as tramadol should be used with particular care in these situations.

Prescribers are advised not to prescribe tramadol for people who are suicidal or prone to addiction , and to use it with caution in people taking tranquilizers or antidepressants, or drinking heavily. If you are struggling with thoughts of harming yourself, or with your use of any medicine, tell your healthcare provider so they can help you find the right treatment and support.

Fatal side effects can occur if you use this medicine with alcohol , or with other drugs that cause drowsiness or slow your breathing. People taking extended-release forms of tramadol should not consume alcohol-containing beverages at the same time. Alcohol is also not advised with immediate-release forms of tramadol.

Serious allergic reactions , including anaphylaxis, have occurred with tramadol, some of which caused death. People who are allergic to other opioids are at higher risk. Other hypersensitivity reactions include itching, hives, narrowing of the airways, swelling of the face and airways (angioedema), and serious skin reactions such as Stevens-Johnson Syndrome. Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction to tramadol (symptoms include hives, difficulty breathing, swelling in your face or throat) or a severe skin reaction (fever, sore throat, burning in your eyes, skin pain, a red or purple skin rash that spreads and causes blistering and peeling).

Tramadol may make pain worse rather than better in some people , an effect called opioid-induced hyperalgesia. Talk to your doctor if the pain you have increases, if you feel more sensitive to pain, or if you have new pain after taking tramadol.

Tramadol extended-release capsules and tablets may complicate a healthcare provider’s clinical assessment of an acute abdominal condition. They are not recommended for people with severe liver or severe kidney impairment.

Taking tramadol during pregnancy may cause life-threatening withdrawal symptoms in the newborn, known as neonatal opioid withdrawal syndrome (NOWS).

Some people are “ultra-rapid metabolizers” of tramadol because of a variation in an enzyme called CYP2D6. Their bodies convert tramadol into its active form faster than usual, which can produce dangerously high opioid levels even at a normal dose. Life-threatening breathing problems and deaths have occurred in children for this reason, including after surgery to remove the tonsils or adenoids. Tramadol should not be given to a child younger than 12 years old, or to anyone younger than 18 years old who has recently had surgery to remove the tonsils or adenoids. Extended-release tramadol should not be given to anyone younger than 18 years.

Tramadol may cause drowsiness or affect your ability to drive or perform hazardous tasks. Do not perform these tasks if tramadol causes confusion, mental impairment, or drowsiness.

Tramadol can cause withdrawal symptom s if stopped suddenly after being taken for a long period of time. Symptoms may include restlessness, drug craving, anxiety, and sweating. Do not stop taking tramadol suddenly without talking to your healthcare provider, as stopping abruptly can cause serious withdrawal symptoms and uncontrolled pain (see How long does tramadol withdrawal last? ).

This is not a complete list of side effects, and others may occur. Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects. You may report side effects to the FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.

Tramadol — Before taking this medicine
Do not take tramadol if you are:

- are allergic to tramadol, to any of the other ingredients in your product, or to other opioids
- have significant breathing problems (respiratory depression)
- have acute or severe asthma and are somewhere you cannot be monitored, or where resuscitation equipment is not available
- have a known or suspected blockage of the stomach or intestines, including paralytic ileus
- have taken a monoamine oxidase inhibitor (MAOI) in the past 14 days (such as isocarboxazid, linezolid, methylene blue injection, phenelzine, or tranylcypromine)
- are younger than 12 years of age
- are younger than 18 years of age and have recently had surgery to remove your tonsils or adenoids.

Seizures have occurred in some people taking tramadol. Your seizure risk may be higher if you have ever had:

- a head injury, epilepsy, or other seizure disorder
- drug or alcohol addiction or
- a metabolic disorder.

To make sure tramadol is safe for you, tell your doctor if you have a history of :

- Head injury or seizures
- Problems urinating
- Liver, kidney, or thyroid problems
- Pancrease or gallbladder problems, including pancreatitis or surgery on your pancreas
- A serious infection
- Abuse of street or prescription drugs, alcohol addiction, opioid overdose, or mental health problems.

Also tell your doctor if you are dehydrated, are eating less or have changed your diet, are on a low-sodium (salt) diet, drink a lot of alcohol, or are planning to have surgery.

Tell your healthcare provider if you are:

- Living in a household where there are small children or someone who has abused street or prescription drugs
- Taking prescription or over-the-counter medicines, vitamins, or herbal supplements. Taking tramadol with certain other medicines can cause serious side effects that could lead to death
- Notice your pain getting worse. If your pain gets worse after you take tramadol, do not take more tramadol without first talking to your doctor. Talk to your doctor if the pain you have increases, if you feel more sensitive to pain, or if you have new pain after taking tramadol.

### Important Information Guiding Use in Pediatric Patients

- Do not give tramadol to a child younger than 12 years of age or extended-release tramadol to anyone under 18 years.
- Do not give tramadol to a child younger than 18 years of age after surgery to remove the tonsils and/or adenoids.
- Avoid giving tramadol to children between 12 and 18 years of age who have risk factors for breathing problems. These include having recently had surgery, obstructive sleep apnea, obesity, severe lung disease, neuromuscular disease, and taking other medicines that can slow breathing.

### Pregnancy

Tramadol may harm an unborn baby and is not usually recommended during pregnancy. Use of tramadol for an extended period of time during pregnancy can cause withdrawal symptoms in your newborn baby that could be life-threatening if not recognized and treated. Neonatal seizures, neonatal withdrawal syndrome, fetal death, and stillbirth have all been reported. Tramadol is also not recommended during or immediately before labor. It should only be used in pregnancy if the benefits outweigh the risks. If you are already taking tramadol and become pregnant, do not stop taking it without talking to your healthcare provider, as stopping suddenly can be harmful to you both.

### Breastfeeding

Breastfeeding is not recommended while taking tramadol, as it may harm your baby. Mothers who are ultra-rapid metabolizers of tramadol can have higher than expected levels of its active form in their breast milk, which can be dangerous for a breastfed infant. Carefully observe infants for increased sleepiness (more than usual), breathing difficulties, or limpness. Seek immediate medical care if you notice these signs.

Tramadol — How should I take tramadol?
Do not change your dose. Take tramadol exactly as prescribed by your healthcare provider. Use the lowest dose possible for the shortest time needed. Tell your doctor if you feel an increased urge to take more of this medicine.

- For acute (short-term) pain, you may only need to take tramadol for a few days . You may have some tramadol left over that you did not use. See disposal information at the bottom of this section for directions on how to safely dispose of tramadol .
- Take your prescribed dose as indicated by your healthcare provider. Take your prescribed dose as indicated by your healthcare provider. After your dose has been settled, immediate-release tramadol is usually taken as 50 mg to 100 mg (one or two 50 mg tablets) every 4 to 6 hours as needed for pain. Do not take more than your prescribed dose, and do not take more than 400 mg of tramadol in one day. For immediate-release tramadol, that is eight 50 mg tablets a day, taken in divided doses, or 80 mL of the 5 mg/mL oral solution. Extended-release tramadol has a lower daily limit of 300 mg, taken as one dose once a day. Always check the strength printed on your own bottle or packet. Do not take tramadol together with any other medicine that contains tramadol.
- If you miss a dose, take your next dose at your usual time.
- Swallow the capsule or tablet whole to avoid exposure to a potentially fatal overdose. Do not crush, chew, break, open, or dissolve.
- Never crush or break a tramadol tablet to inhale the powder or mix it into a liquid to inject the drug into your vein. This practice has resulted in death.
- May be taken with or without food.

### If you take the oral solution

Tramadol oral solution is measured in millilitres (mL), not milligrams (mg), and contains 5 mg of tramadol in every 1 mL. Confusing the two units can cause a fatal overdose. Always measure your dose with a graduated oral syringe or measuring cup marked in mL, which your pharmacy can supply, and never with a household teaspoon or tablespoon. If your prescription is written in milligrams, ask your pharmacist to confirm how many millilitres that is before you take your first dose.

### Notes

You may have withdrawal symptoms if you stop using tramadol suddenly. Ask your doctor before stopping the medicine.

Ask your healthcare provider about medicines like naloxone or nalmefene that can be used in an emergency to reverse an opioid overdose.

Do not keep leftover tramadol. Just one dose can cause death if someone uses it accidentally or improperly. Ask your pharmacist where to locate a drug take-back disposal program. If there is no take-back program, mix the leftover medicine with cat litter or coffee grounds in a sealed plastic bag and throw the bag in the trash.

Call your healthcare provider if the dose you are taking does not control your pain.

If you have been taking tramadol regularly, do not stop taking tramadol without talking to your healthcare provider.

Tramadol — Dosing Information
### Adult Tramadol Dosage for Pain

Immediate-release formulations (tablets and oral solution)

- Treatment usually starts at 25 mg a day, increasing by 25 mg every 3 days to reach 100 mg a day (25 mg four times a day). Doses of 25 mg are measured using the oral solution, as immediate-release tramadol tablets are only available as 50 mg.
- For the immediate-release tablets, a common starting dose is one 50 mg tablet a day to see how well you tolerate it, or the dose prescribed by your doctor.
- If needed, the total daily dose can then be increased by 50 mg every 3 days as tolerated, to reach 200 mg a day (one 50 mg tablet four times a day).
- After this titration, 50 mg to 100 mg (one or two 50 mg tablets) can be taken every 4 to 6 hours as needed for pain.
- The maximum is 400 mg a day: eight 50 mg tablets in divided doses, or 80 mL of the 5 mg/mL oral solution.

Extended-release capsules and tablets

- Tramadol ER 100 mg once a day (one 100 mg tablet or capsule) at approximately the same time each day.
- Can be increased by 100 mg every 5 days if needed, to a maximum of 300 mg a day, taken as one dose once a day.
- Extended-release tramadol is not intended for as-needed pain relief, and should not be taken with any other tramadol product.

Dosage in kidney or liver impairment

- Severe kidney impairment: for immediate-release tramadol, doses are spaced 12 hours apart, and the maximum is 200 mg a day (four 50 mg tablets, given as two 100 mg doses 12 hours apart).
- Severe liver impairment: for immediate-release tramadol, the recommended dose is 50 mg (one 50 mg tablet) every 12 hours, which is 100 mg a day.
- Extended-release tramadol is not recommended for people with severe kidney or severe liver impairment.

Dosage in older adults

- No separate maximum dose applies on the basis of age, but tramadol should be started at the low end of the dosing range and increased slowly, with frequent review for drowsiness and breathing problems.
- Side effects that led people to stop treatment were more common over the age of 75, mostly stomach and bowel effects, such as constipation. Kidney function also tends to decline with age, which affects how tramadol is cleared, so your doctor may take this into account when choosing your dose.

Tramadol — What happens if I miss a dose?
Since tramadol is used for pain, you are unlikely to miss a dose. Skip any missed dose if it is almost time for your next dose. Do not use two doses at one time.

Tramadol — What happens if I overdose?
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222. An overdose can be fatal, especially in a child or other person using the medicine without a prescription. Overdose symptoms may include severe drowsiness, pinpoint pupils, slow breathing, or no breathing.

Your doctor may recommend you get naloxone (a medicine to reverse an opioid overdose) and keep it with you at all times. A person caring for you can give naloxone if you stop breathing or don't wake up. Your caregiver must still get emergency medical help and may need to perform CPR (cardiopulmonary resuscitation) on you while waiting for help to arrive.

Anyone can buy naloxone from a pharmacy or local health department. Make sure any person caring for you knows where you keep naloxone and how to use it.

Tramadol — What should I avoid while taking tramadol?
Do not drink alcohol or use prescription or over-the-counter medicines that contain alcohol. Dangerous side effects or death could occur.

Avoid driving or hazardous activities until you know how tramadol will affect you. Dizziness or drowsiness can cause falls, accidents, or severe injuries.

Tramadol — What other drugs will affect tramadol?
You may have breathing problems or withdrawal symptoms if you start or stop taking certain other medicines. Tramadol is broken down by two liver enzymes called CYP3A4 and CYP2D6, and medicines that block or speed up these enzymes change how much tramadol and how much of its active form are in your body. Because starting one of these medicines and stopping one both shift the balance, tell your doctor whenever you begin or finish a course of an antibiotic, antifungal medication, heart or blood pressure medication, seizure medication, or medicine to treat HIV or hepatitis C.

Many other drugs can be dangerous when used with tramadol. Tell your doctor if you also use:

- medicine for allergies, asthma, blood pressure, motion sickness, irritable bowel, or overactive bladder
- other opioid medicines
- buprenorphine, butorphanol, nalbuphine, or pentazocine. These opioids work differently and can block tramadol's pain-relieving effect or bring on sudden withdrawal symptoms, so they are generally not taken with tramadol
- a benzodiazepine sedative like Valium, Klonopin, or Xanax
- sleep medicine, muscle relaxers, or other drugs that make you drowsy
- drugs that affect serotonin, such as antidepressants, stimulants, or medicine for migraines or Parkinson's disease
- carbamazepine, which is not recommended with tramadol because it can significantly reduce tramadol's pain-relieving effect and add to the risk of seizures
- digoxin or warfarin, which need extra monitoring when taken with tramadol.

This list is not complete. Other drugs may interact with tramadol, including prescription and over-the-counter medicines, vitamins, and herbal products. Not all possible interactions are listed here.

Tramadol — Storage
Store at room temperature away from moisture and heat and out of reach of children. Do not share with others.

Trazodone — What is trazodone?
Trazodone is an antidepressant that belongs to a group of drugs called serotonin receptor antagonists and reuptake inhibitors (SARIs). While trazodone is not a true member of the selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs) class of antidepressants, it does still share many properties of the SSRIs.

Trazodone is used to treat major depressive disorder .

It may help to improve your mood, appetite, and energy level as well as decrease anxiety and insomnia related to depression.

Trazodone works by helping to restore the balance of a certain natural chemical (serotonin) in the brain.

Trazodone — Warnings
You should not use trazodone if you are allergic to it, or if you are being treated with methylene blue injection.

Do not use this medicine if you have taken an MAO inhibitor in the past 14 days. A dangerous drug interaction could occur. MAO inhibitors include isocarboxazid, linezolid, phenelzine, tranylcypromine and others.

Some young people have thoughts about suicide when first taking an antidepressant. Your doctor will need to check your progress at regular visits while you are using trazodone. Your family or other caregivers should also be alert to changes in your mood or symptoms.

Report any new or worsening symptoms to your doctor, such as: mood or behavior changes, anxiety, panic attacks, trouble sleeping, or if you feel impulsive, irritable, agitated, hostile, aggressive, restless, hyperactive (mentally or physically), more depressed, or have thoughts about suicide or hurting yourself.

Do not give this medicine to anyone younger than 18 years old without the advice of a doctor. Trazodone is not approved for use in children.

Trazodone — Before taking this medicine
You should not use trazodone if you are allergic to it.

Do not use this medicine if you have used an MAO inhibitor in the past 14 days. A dangerous drug interaction could occur. MAO inhibitors include isocarboxazid, linezolid, methylene blue injection, phenelzine, tranylcypromine, and others.

After you stop taking trazodone, you must wait at least 14 days before you start taking an MAOI.

Tell your doctor if you also take stimulant medicine, opioid medicine, herbal products, or medicine for depression, mental illness, Parkinson's disease, migraine headaches, serious infections, or prevention of nausea and vomiting. An interaction with trazodone could cause a serious condition called serotonin syndrome.

To make sure this medicine is safe for you, tell your doctor if you have ever had:

- liver or kidney disease;
- heart disease, or a recent heart attack ;
- a bleeding or blood clotting disorder;
- seizures or epilepsy ;
- narrow-angle glaucoma ;
- long QT syndrome ;
- drug addiction or suicidal thoughts; or
- bipolar disorder (manic depression ).

Some young people have thoughts about suicide when first taking an antidepressant. Your doctor should check your progress at regular visits. Your family or other caregivers should also be alert to changes in your mood or symptoms.

Taking trazodone during pregnancy could harm the baby, but stopping the medicine may not be safe for you. Do not start or stop this medicine without asking your doctor.

If you are pregnant, your name may be listed on a pregnancy registry to track the effects of trazodone on the baby.

It may not be safe to breast-feed while using this medicine. Ask your doctor about any risk.

Trazodone is not approved for use by anyone younger than 18 years old.

Trazodone — How should I take trazodone?
Take trazodone exactly as prescribed by your doctor. Follow all directions on your prescription label and read all medication guides or instruction sheets. Your doctor may occasionally change your dose.

Take trazodone after a meal or a snack.

It may take up to 2 weeks before your symptoms improve. Keep using the medication as directed and tell your doctor if your symptoms do not improve.

You should not stop using this medicine suddenly, or you could have unpleasant symptoms (such as dizziness, vomiting, agitation, sweating, confusion, numbness, tingling, or electric shock feelings). Ask your doctor how to safely stop using this medicine.

Store at room temperature away from moisture, heat, and light. If you are using the oral solution, throw away any unused medicine 20 days after first opening the bottle.

Trazodone — Dosing information
Usual Adult Dose for Depression:

Initial dose: 150 mg orally per day in divided doses; this may be increased by 50 mg orally per day every 3 to 4 days Maximum dose: -Inpatients: 600 mg/day -Outpatients: 400 mg/day Comments: -Patients should be screened for a personal/family history of bipolar disorder, mania, or hypomania prior to initiating treatment. -Patients should be monitored for withdrawal symptoms when discontinuing therapy. -After an adequate response has been reached, dosage may be gradually reduced depending on therapeutic response. -If drowsiness develops, a major portion of the daily dose may be administered at bedtime or a reduction of dosage may be necessary. -This drug should be taken shortly after a meal or light snack. Use: Treatment of major depressive disorder (MDD)

Trazodone — What happens if I miss a dose?
Take the medicine as soon as you can, but skip the missed dose if it is almost time for your next dose. Do not take two doses at one time.

Trazodone — What happens if I overdose?
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222. An overdose can be fatal when trazodone is taken with alcohol, barbiturates such as phenobarbital, or sedatives such as diazepam (Valium).

Overdose symptoms may include extreme drowsiness, vomiting , penis erection that is painful or prolonged, fast or pounding heartbeat, seizure (black-out or convulsions), or breathing that slows or stops.

Trazodone — What to avoid
Do not drink alcohol. Dangerous side effects or death could occur.

Ask your doctor before taking a nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug (NSAID) such as aspirin, ibuprofen, naproxen, Advil, Aleve, Motrin, and others. Using an NSAID with trazodone may cause you to bruise or bleed easily.

Avoid driving or hazardous activity until you know how this medicine will affect you. Your reactions could be impaired.

Avoid getting up too fast from a sitting or lying position, or you may feel dizzy.

Trazodone — Trazodone side effects
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction to trazodone: hives ; difficulty breathing; swelling of your face, lips, tongue, or throat.

Stop taking trazodone and call your doctor at once if you have a penis erection that is painful or lasts 6 hours or longer. This is a medical emergency and could lead to a serious condition that must be corrected with surgery.

Report any new or worsening symptoms to your doctor, such as: mood or behavior changes, anxiety , panic attacks , trouble sleeping, or if you feel impulsive, irritable, agitated, hostile, aggressive, restless, hyperactive (mentally or physically), more depressed, or have thoughts about suicide or hurting yourself.

Call your doctor at once if you have:

- fast or pounding heartbeats, fluttering in your chest, shortness of breath, and sudden dizziness (like you might pass out);
- slow heartbeats;
- unusual thoughts or behavior;
- easy bruising, unusual bleeding; or
- low levels of sodium in the body - headache, confusion, slurred speech, severe weakness, vomiting, loss of coordination, feeling unsteady.

Seek medical attention right away if you have symptoms of serotonin syndrome, such as: agitation , hallucinations, fever, sweating, shivering, fast heart rate , muscle stiffness, twitching, loss of coordination, nausea , vomiting, or diarrhea .

Common trazodone side effects may include:

- drowsiness, dizziness, tiredness;
- swelling;
- weight loss;
- blurred vision;
- diarrhea, constipation ; or
- stuffy nose.

This is not a complete list of side effects and others may occur. Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects. You may report side effects to FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.

Trazodone — What other drugs will affect trazodone?
Using trazodone with other drugs that make you drowsy can worsen this effect. Ask your doctor before using opioid medication, a sleeping pill, a muscle relaxer, or medicine for anxiety or seizures.

Tell your doctor about all your current medicines. Many drugs can interact with trazodone, especially:

- any other antidepressants;
- phenytoin ;
- St. John's wort ;
- tramadol ;
- a diuretic or "water pill";
- medicine to treat anxiety, mood disorders, or mental illness such as schizophrenia ;
- a blood thinner - warfarin , Coumadin , Jantoven ; or
- migraine headache medicine - sumatriptan , Imitrex , Maxalt , Treximet , and others.

This list is not complete and many other drugs may interact with trazodone. This includes prescription and over-the-counter medicines, vitamins , and herbal products . Not all possible drug interactions are listed here.

Venlafaxine — What is venlafaxine?
Venlafaxine is used in adults to treat major depressive disorder . Venlafaxine extended-release (long-acting) capsules are also used to treat generalized anxiety disorder (GAD; excessive worrying that is difficult to control), social anxiety disorder (extreme fear of interacting with others or performing in front of others that interferes with normal life), and panic disorder (sudden, unexpected attacks of extreme fear and worry about these attacks).

Venlafaxine belongs to a class of medications called selective serotonin and norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs). Venlafaxine works by increasing the amounts of serotonin and norepinephrine, natural substances in the brain that help maintain mental balance.

Venlafaxine — Warnings
You should not take venlafaxine if you have uncontrolled narrow-angle glaucoma .

Do not use venlafaxine within 7 days before or 14 days after you have used an MAO inhibitor, such as isocarboxazid , linezolid , methylene blue injection, phenelzine , or tranylcypromine .

Some young people have thoughts about suicide when first taking an antidepressant. Stay alert to changes in your mood or symptoms. Report any new or worsening symptoms to your doctor, such as: mood or behavioral changes, anxiety, panic attacks, trouble sleeping, or if you feel impulsive, irritable, agitated, hostile, aggressive, restless, hyperactive (mentally or physically), more depressed, or have thoughts about suicide or hurting yourself.

Do not stop using venlafaxine without first talking to your doctor.

Do not give this medicine to anyone younger than 18 years old without the advice of a doctor. Venlafaxine is not FDA approved for use in children.

Venlafaxine — Before taking this medicine
You should not take venlafaxine if you are allergic to it or to desvenlafaxine ( Pristiq ).

Do not use venlafaxine if you have used an MAO inhibitor in the past 14 days. A dangerous drug interaction could occur. MAO inhibitors include isocarboxazid, linezolid, methylene blue injection, phenelzine, and tranylcypromine.

Tell your doctor if you also use stimulant medicine, opioid medicine, herbal products , or medicine for depression, mental illness, Parkinson's disease , migraine headaches, serious infections, or prevention of nausea and vomiting . An interaction with venlafaxine could cause a serious condition called serotonin syndrome.

To make sure venlafaxine is safe for you, tell your doctor if you have ever had:

- bipolar disorder (manic depression);
- heart disease , high blood pressure , high cholesterol or triglycerides, or a stroke;
- diabetes;
- lung disease;
- glaucoma ;
- a thyroid disorder;
- a seizure;
- sexual problems;
- bleeding problems;
- low blood levels of sodium; or
- liver or kidney disease.

Some young people may have increased suicidal thoughts when first starting a medicine to treat depression. Stay alert to changes in your mood or symptoms. Your family or caregivers should also watch for sudden changes in your behavior.

Taking this medicine during pregnancy could harm the baby, and taking it in the month before delivery may increase your risk of severe bleeding after childbirth. However, stopping the medicine may not be safe for you. Do not start or stop venlafaxine without asking your doctor.

If you are pregnant, your name may be listed on a pregnancy registry to track the effects of venlafaxine on the baby.

Ask a doctor if it is safe to breastfeed while using this medicine.

Venlafaxine — How should I take venlafaxine?
Take venlafaxine as prescribed by your doctor. Follow all directions on your prescription label and read all medication guides or instruction sheets. Your doctor may occasionally change your dose.

Take with food at the same time each day.

Swallow the extended-release capsule or tablet whole and do not crush, chew, break, or open it.

If you cannot swallow a capsule whole, open it and mix the medicine with applesauce. Swallow the mixture right away without chewing.

Tell your doctor if you have any changes in sexual function, such as loss of interest in sex, trouble having an orgasm, or (in men) problems with erections or ejaculation. Some sexual problems can be treated.

Your symptoms may not improve for up to several weeks. Do not stop using venlafaxine suddenly, or you could have severe and long-lasting unpleasant symptoms (such as agitation , confusion, tingling or electric shock feelings, aggression, violent behavior, suicidal thoughts, vision changes, or increased blood pressure). Ask your doctor before stopping the medicine.

Your blood pressure and cholesterol levels may need to be checked often.

This medicine may cause false results on a drug-screening urine test. Tell the laboratory staff that you use venlafaxine.

Store at room temperature away from moisture and heat.

Venlafaxine — Dosing Information
Usual Adult Dose for Depression:

Immediate release: -Initial dose: 37.5 mg orally twice a day OR 25 mg orally 3 times a day -Maintenance dose: 75 to 150 mg orally per day, given in divided doses -Maximum dose: ---Moderately depressed outpatients: 225 mg/day ---Severely depressed inpatients: 375 mg/day Extended release: -Initial dose: 75 mg orally once a day -Maintenance dose: 75 to 225 mg orally once a day -Maximum dose: ---Moderately depressed outpatients: 225 mg/day ---Severely depressed inpatients: 375 mg/day Comments: -The daily dosage of immediate-release formulations may be divided into 2 or 3 doses/day. -Healthcare providers should consider initial extended release doses of 37.5 mg orally once a day for 4 to 7 days (before increasing the dose to 75 mg/day) in patients who may require additional time to adjust to the drug. -Doses may be increased in daily increments of up to 75 mg orally at intervals of no less than 4 days. -Patients should be periodically reassessed for the continued need of maintenance treatment and for the appropriate dose of treatment. -Use of doses exceeding 225 mg/day is limited.

Usual Adult Dose for Generalized Anxiety Disorder:

Extended release: -Initial dose: 75 mg orally once a day -Maintenance dose: 75 to 225 mg orally once a day -Maximum dose: 225 mg/day Comments: -Healthcare providers should consider initial extended release doses of 37.5 mg orally once a day for 4 to 7 days (before increasing the dose to 75 mg/day) in patients who may require additional time to adjust to the drug. -Doses may be increased in daily increments of up to 75 mg orally at intervals of no less than 4 days.

Usual Adult Dose for Social Anxiety Disorder:

Extended release: 75 mg orally once a day Comment: There is no evidence that doses greater than 75 mg/day confer additional benefit.

Usual Adult Dose for Panic Disorder:

Extended release: -Initial dose: 37.5 mg orally once a day for 7 days, then 75 mg orally once a day thereafter -Maintenance dose: 75 to 225 mg orally once a day -Maximum dose: 225 mg/day Comment: Doses may be increased in daily increments of 75 mg orally at intervals of no less than 7 days.

Venlafaxine — What happens if I miss a dose?
Take the medicine as soon as you can, but skip the missed dose if it is almost time for your next dose. Do not take two doses at one time.

Venlafaxine — What happens if I overdose?
Seek emergency medical attention or call the Poison Help line at 1-800-222-1222.

Venlafaxine — What should I avoid while taking venlafaxine?
Do not drink alcohol.

Ask your doctor before taking a nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug (NSAID) such as aspirin , ibuprofen , naproxen , Advil , Aleve , Motrin , and others. Using an NSAID with venlafaxine may cause you to bruise or bleed easily.

Avoid driving or hazardous activity until you know how venlafaxine will affect you. Your reactions could be impaired.

Venlafaxine — Venlafaxine side effects
Get emergency medical help if you have signs of an allergic reaction to venlafaxine: hives; difficult breathing; swelling of your face, lips, tongue, or throat.

Tell your doctor right away if you have new or sudden changes in mood or behavior, including new or worse depression or anxiety, panic attacks , trouble sleeping, or if you feel impulsive, irritable, agitated, hostile, aggressive, restless, more active or talkative, or have thoughts about suicide or hurting yourself.

Venlafaxine may cause serious side effects. Call your doctor at once if you have:

- blurred vision, eye pain or redness, seeing halos around lights;
- cough , chest tightness, trouble breathing;
- a seizure (convulsions);
- unusual bleeding - nosebleeds, bleeding gums, abnormal vaginal bleeding, any bleeding that will not stop;
- low blood sodium - headache , confusion, problems with thinking or memory, weakness, feeling unsteady; or
- severe nervous system reaction - very stiff (rigid) muscles, high fever , sweating, confusion, fast or uneven heartbeats, tremors, feeling like you might pass out.

Seek medical attention right away if you have symptoms of serotonin syndrome, such as: agitation, hallucinations, fever, sweating, shivering, fast heart rate , muscle stiffness, twitching, loss of coordination, nausea, vomiting, or diarrhea .

Common venlafaxine side effects may include:

- headache, dizziness, drowsiness , tiredness;
- ringing in your ears, feeling anxious, nervous, or jittery;
- sleep problems, unusual dreams;
- tremors;
- fast heartbeats;
- blurred vision;
- nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, constipation ;
- changes in weight or appetite;
- dry mouth, yawning;
- increased sweating; or
- sexual problems.

This is not a complete list of side effects and others may occur. Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects. You may report side effects to FDA at 1-800-FDA-1088.

Venlafaxine — What other drugs will affect venlafaxine?
Using venlafaxine with other drugs that make you drowsy can worsen this effect. Ask your doctor before using opioid medication, a sleeping pill, a muscle relaxer, or medicine for anxiety or seizures .

Tell your doctor about all your current medicines. Many drugs can affect venlafaxine, especially:

- any other antidepressant, or other medicines containing venlafaxine or desvenlafaxine;
- amphetamines;
- fentanyl ;
- cimetidine ;
- tramadol ;
- St. John's wort , tryptophan (sometimes called L-tryptophan);
- diet pills, weight loss medicine (such as phentermine );
- a blood thinner ( warfarin , Coumadin , Jantoven );
- medicine to treat mood disorders, thought disorders, or mental illness - buspirone , lithium , and many others; or
- migraine headache medicine - sumatriptan , zolmitriptan , and others.

This list is not complete and many other drugs may interact with venlafaxine. This includes prescription and over-the-counter medicines, vitamins , and herbal products. Not all possible drug interactions are listed here.
